2.4

Survey definition process

The HLWAS survey definition process consisted of a broad effort collecting relevant inputs and information,
followed by a process of determining and refining the recommendations. The overall process is described
here.
2.4.1

Meetings and avenues for input

1. Committee meetings. – The primary avenue for discussion among the committee was regular video
calls, typically one hour in duration. Weekly telecons started in February 2024 and continued through
the spring, with a detailed discussion of community white papers and science pitches. In the summer,
these calls were less frequent, with work being done in working groups. In fall 2024 the full committee telecons returned to a weekly cadence, with additional (twice-weekly) meetings in November
and December to finalize recommendations and report. Committee discussions comprised a total of
approximately 40 meetings over 10 months.
2. Community white papers and science pitches. – A great deal of input to the CCS definition process
came from white papers and science pitches submitted by the community, as discussed in detail below. In winter and spring 2024, the committee read and discussed all submitted white papers. Each
white paper was assigned to committee members who led a summary and discussion of the paper in
a committee meeting. Likewise, science pitches relevant to HLWAS were considered in committee
discussions.
3. Working groups. – In summer 2024, the committee was divided into four working groups that examined
key areas of the survey trade space. One of these assessed the details of a potential wide tier in our
multi-tiered strategy, while the other three groups addressed aspects of the medium and deep tiers:
depth/area/bands, field placement, and observing parameters (cadence, roll angles, etc.)

4

4. Community Town Halls. – The HLWASDC, along with the other CCS committees, participated in
multiple Town Halls to provide updates to the community on the survey definition process. There was
one such presentation at the Roman science conference in Pasadena, CA in July 2024, followed by two
virtual Town Hall meetings in August and September 2024.
5. Engagement with PITs. – The formal engagement of the HLWASDC with the two PITs took place
in early September 2024. Requests for input, in the form of a list of questions, were sent to each
PIT, with responses from the PITs in the form of slides and presentations to the committee, received
in mid-October 2024. The PIT requests, and responses, discussed in more detail in Appendix B.
Throughout the process, less formal engagement with PITs took place via the PIT members serving on
the HLWASDC who provided updates and feedback to the committee discussions.
2.4.2

Process for determining recommendations

The process of determining and refining the committee’s final recommendations started with discussion of the
responses from the PITs to the committee’s questions. The PITs each provided a set of recommendations for
an observing strategy (for imaging and spectroscopy, respectively) that could be carried out in the available
in-guide observing time. The committee then took these responses as a point from which to examine the
trade spaces around various survey parameters, which is discussed in detail in the next section. This trade
space was ultimately narrowed down to a set of point designs that were used to evaluate and choose the
recommended in-guide survey and the options for overguide and underguide.

3
3.1

Science motivation
Science Requirements and Figures of Merit for Cosmology

One of the primary motivations for the HLWAS is to provide tight constraints on cosmological parameters and
stringent tests of cosmological models. Figures of Merit (FoMs) for cosmological experiments are typically
defined in terms of the forecast uncertainties on cosmological parameters, and they are often chosen to scale
as an inverse variance, so that the FoM scales positively with the data volume (e.g., the fraction of sky
covered) if all other parameters are held fixed.
The value of an FoM depends on what space of cosmological parameters is being considered, including
parameters that are not part of the FoM itself but must be marginalized over when inferring constraints
on the target parameters. It also depends on what systematics are being considered (often described by
nuisance parameters that must also be marginalized over), on what external data are presumed available
to set additional constraints, and on modeling assumptions such as the range of scales that can be used
before theoretical systematics become dominant. Despite these complications, sensibly chosen FoMs will
usually give similar guidance on how to make trade decisions to optimize performance — e.g., depth vs. area
— provided one makes consistent assumptions while examining these trades. For this reason, the Roman
Science Requirements Document (SRD) defined requirements relative to the performance expected for a
reference survey, covering 2000 deg2 in 4-band imaging and grism spectroscopy with specified depth.
The trickiest issue in using an FoM to guide survey design is that performance may be limited by systematics that are either unknown or difficult to quantify at a level that can be incorporated into an FoM
calculation. A calculation that considers statistical errors alone tends to favor “data volume”, e.g., number of
galaxy redshifts or shape measurements. Systematics control often requires higher “data quality”, e.g., depth
or filter coverage, making each measurement more expensive. Drawing on the expertise of the High Latitude Imaging Survey (HLIS) and High Latitude Spectroscopic Survey (HLSS) Project Infrastructure Teams
(PITs)2 , we have tried to balance these considerations appropriately in our design recommendations.
2 Details of the Roman PITs are available at: https://roman.gsfc.nasa.gov/science/Roman teamlist pit.html.
In this report, we will refer to the “Maximizing Cosmological Science with the Roman High Latitude Imaging Survey” PIT (PI O. Doré)
and the “Project Infrastructure for the Roman Galaxy Redshift Survey” PIT (PI Y. Wang) as the HLIS PIT and HLSS PIT, respectively.

5

For weak lensing (WL), the FoM adopted in the SRD is the inverse square of the 1-σ fractional error on
σ8 , considered to be a single free parameter that linearly scales the amplitude of matter fluctuations at all
redshifts. The SRD FoM is based on cosmic shear only, and because the cosmic shear power spectrum scales
with σ82 , the defined FoM is effectively a quantification of (SNR)2 , where SNR is the aggregate signal-to-noise
ratio of the cosmic shear power spectrum measurement. There are important subtleties to SNR, however, such
as the correlated statistical errors at small angular scales that arise from non-linear evolution of structure. We
expect that Roman WL data will also provide important constraints on cosmological parameters through
galaxy–galaxy lensing, lensing by galaxy clusters, and cross-correlation with lensing maps of the cosmic
microwave background (CMB lensing). These different analysis approaches tend to pull in similar but not
identical directions in trade decisions.
For the galaxy redshift survey (GRS), the SRD adopts one FoM for baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO)
and one for redshift-space distortion (RSD). BAO measure the angular diameter distance DA and the Hubble
parameter H as functions of redshift. The BAO FoM is the inverse of the product of the 1-sigma statistical
errors on an overall scaling of DA (z) and H(z) over the redshift range 1 < z < 3. The RSD FoM is the
inverse square of the fractional error on the growth rate parameter σm (z)f (z) over the same redshift range.
These two FoMs have similar scaling with survey area and galaxy density because BAO and RSD constraints
are both derived from the galaxy power spectrum on comparable scales.
The requirements defined in the SRD include some margin relative to the expected performance of the
reference survey (itself an evolution of the notional survey in Spergel et al. 2015). That margin is allocated
to several potential contributions in each case, with a factor of 0.85 coming from a lower survey area of 1700
deg2 vs. 2000 deg2 . From the point of view of the HLWAS design, therefore, it is reasonable to take the
requirement on the forecast of the FoM to be 0.85 of the forecast for the reference survey.
The reference survey was expected to require approximately 600 days of observing time. Although the
in-guide time for the HLWAS is lower, at 520 days, we expect our recommended strategies for the imaging
and spectroscopic surveys to exceed the FoM requirements (including the 0.85 factor) and even exceed the
forecast FoM of the reference survey itself. This is partly a consequence of better observatory performance
relative to the SRD assumptions, in particular lower slew+settle times and lower read noise in the detectors.
It is also a consequence of design choices that yield higher statistical performance for a given amount of
observing time — basically, trading greater area for shallower depth and/or less filter coverage. We believe
that our recommended strategies will still allow control of systematics at the level needed to meet the science
requirements. We give further details of the trade decisions and forecasts in Sections 4 and 5 below.

3.2

Summary of White Papers, Science Pitches, and other community feedback

The High Latitude Wide Area Survey core community survey definition committee analyzed the community
input contributed in 38 submitted white papers and 52 science pitches. We also considered three additional
white papers previously submitted to the Early Definition Astrophysics Survey that are relevant in the context
of the HLWAS footprint placement. We include the questions used in our discussion in Appendix A.
The science scope of the 41 white papers touches all scales of Astrophysics and can be distributed among
the following broad categories: cosmology (6), galaxies (10), local Universe (7), solar system (3), and synergies with other surveys (15). A non-exhaustive list of topics addressed by the white papers and the pitches
includes large-scale structure in the early universe, CMB lensing, cosmic infrared background fluctuations,
cosmic distance ladder, low-mass strong lensing systems, measurements of dark matter substructure, environmental dependence of galaxy quenching, the formation of bulges, the characteristics of proto-clusters,
studies of the high redshift universe including the search for z > 10 galaxies and the discovery of the earliest
quasars and AGN, measuring the structure of galaxies’ dark matter halos through their globular cluster populations, detection of stellar streams and halos, discovering substellar populations characterizing substructure
and satellite galaxies in the MW’s halo, and characterizing the orbits and surface compositions of small solar
system bodies.
We are grateful to the PITs for their responsiveness and valuable inputs to the committee’s work.

6

Most submissions present science cases that would benefit from modifications or extensions to the baseline Design Reference Mission. The proposals include suggestions for area, depth, filters, cadences, and
footprint location, both for the imaging and the spectroscopy surveys. Some proposals suggest observing
strategies and exposure times, while others give no details on the survey design needed to achieve the science
cases. A few cases do not address survey properties but express concerns about other aspects of the mission,
such as data archival, data processing, the definition of optimal figures-of-merit, or the need to observe standard reference fields for calibration. Some of the suggested scenarios would be difficult to implement in a
reference mission and were considered more appropriate for future submissions to the General Astrophysics
program. In general, the white papers give more details on survey design than the shorter science pitches. In
the following, we summarize the suggestions for survey properties addressed in the white papers.
Area: A number of white papers suggest an increase of the reference wide survey area, trading off with a
decrease in number of filters or number of passes, benefiting different science cases. It is argued that a larger
area improves dark energy cosmological constraints from WL, and also increases the number of massive
clusters. It also provides the opportunity to measure distances to thousands of galaxies out to hundreds
of Mpc for independent measures of the Hubble constant, and provides tremendous impact on astrometry,
star–galaxy separation, and other aspects of the nearby universe. Some examples of proposed coverages are:
• Full sky coverage at the reference depth, yielding high-resolution images of nearly every object in the
sky. If observed at multiple epochs (at least two passes), it would enable the measurement of the proper
motions of MW stars. Full sky coverage could be achieved in the in-guide time if the survey was carried
out in the single broad filter (F146) filter, at the cost of undersampled imaging and overhead-dominated
operations.
• Wide area of 18,000 deg2, matching Rubin observatory’s LSST footprint, again in the F146 filter.
The synergy with LSST has many benefits, such as improving photo-z estimation, Rubin deblending,
yielding a sample of faint resolved stellar populations, etc.
Multiple tiers are scenarios where parts of the HLWAS footprint are covered with different strategies, such
as different depths or usage of filters. A suggestion is an 18,000 deg2 wide tier observed in a single band,
accompanied by a 1,000 deg2 medium tier observed in the four nominal bands for controlling systematics.
The two tiers would fit into the in-guide time.
Deep tier: The DRM contains a deep component designed for calibration of the cosmological measurements.
Some of the white papers make suggestions regarding location and properties of the deep tier with the goal
of enhancing its scientific potential. Some examples are:
• Covering the Euclid Deep Fields, possibly adding K-band observations. This would include covering
the EDF-North in the northern ecliptic pole, which is far from the reference footprint.
• Revisiting small areas of the footprint of the reference survey in all available 8 imaging passbands,
resulting in a set of deeper fields of various depths and sizes. These deep fields would enable a broad
variety of galaxy evolution studies.
• Overlapping the LSST Deep Drilling Fields.
• Placing the deep fields in the continuous viewing zone area (within 36 deg of the ecliptic poles) to
enable a diverse range of roll angles, and thus a diversity of dispersion directions for better mitigation
of overlap between spectra of unrelated objects.
F213 filter (K-band): There are several suggestions to include the longer wavelength passband to enable
science cases across a broad range of scales, from the detection of large samples of high-z bright galaxies and proto-clusters to the characterization of dust-obscured transient populations, ultra-cool dwarfs, and
extragalactic star clusters. Proposed strategies include:
7

• Performing the reference survey, adding the additional filter to the reference set of 4 filters.
• Replacing the shorter wavelength Y-band with the K-band.
• Selecting specific regions of the sky to be observed in the K-band.
Number of epochs is another property of survey design. There are proposals of multiple visits of the HLWAS,
possibly trading off with the number of filters. The suggestions can be grouped in the two following contexts:
• Transients and fast-moving objects. Multiple epochs are needed to detect transient effects for various
science cases, such as improving the distance ladder with observations of AGB variable stars, characterizing transient populations, or for proper motions and discovery of solar system small bodies.
• Grism spectroscopy. Observations from multiple points of the orbit deliver multiple position angles
and thus a diversity of dispersion directions. One specific science case mentioned is the need to distinguish the spectra from source galaxies and foreground lenses in observations of Extreme emission line
galaxies at cosmic noon.
Dithering: One paper mentions modifications in dithering in the context of optimizing the HLWAS for low
surface brightness astronomy. The HLWAS provides an opportunity to explore new science in the LSB
Universe. It has adequate depth, filters, long exposures, high imaging resolution (provided it stays clear from
sources of contamination and applies LSB-dedicated data reduction). It would need, however, larger dither
steps to minimize reflections in the optical path.
Custom surveys: There was a broad range of suggestions to extend a reference survey by including custom
surveys. The committee considers that some of them would be more appropriate for the General Astrophysics
Surveys (GAS) program. Some examples are:
• A survey of 100 deg2 observed in the two short-wavelength filters F087 and F106 to detect low-mass
dark matter substructure through strong lensing.
• A 500 deg2 survey fully covering one object in the local volume (Andromeda galaxy and the Sculptor
group were suggested), in two bands for colors, in two epochs for proper motions and deeper than the
reference survey for characterization of ultra-faint dwarfs, stellar streams, satellite populations, etc.
• Adding an area at low ecliptic latitudes to be revisited with a high cadence would enable the discovery
of Solar System small bodies and also, with an appropriate choice of filters, some characterization of
TNOs.
• The “Roman Cosmic Noon Survey”, a proposal for a 10 deg2 deep spectroscopic survey (8h per pointing) in the continuous viewing zone to study proto-clusters in their cosmic web environment. The
proposal also includes imaging and has legacy value beyond the main science case.
Synergies: A number of papers advocate the benefits of overlapping HLWAS with other surveys or wellestablished astronomical fields. Besides the already mentioned Rubin’s LSST and Euclid, other cases specifically mentioned are: the SPT-3G survey (enabling cross-correlation with CMB), HERA radio telescope
(cross-correlations with 21cm to constrain reionization), the Stripe 82-XL (with multi-wavelength coverage
including X-ray), and the future CASTOR space telescope. There are also several papers devoted to synergies with Subaru in the context of a set of 100 Subaru nights committed to Roman–Subaru synergistic
observations. In this context a few suggestions are made:
• HLWAS overlapping with the Subaru HSC Deep areas (27 deg2 ) using Roman spectroscopy and imaging (including K-band) for large-scale structures and galaxy formation studies.
• A 100 deg2 survey on the HSC-W-HectoMap Subaru survey for better identifying low-z interlopers.
8

• HLWAS footprint accessible from Subaru to enable a follow-up HSC survey in 16 Medium-Band filters,
and a follow-up with the future ULTIMATE-Subaru instrument.

4

Trade Considerations and Recommended In-Guide Survey

We assume from the outset that the HLWAS will include a deep tier, taking roughly 10% of the observing
time, and one or more tiers that cover a much larger area. The deep tier is required for calibration observations
that enable scientific exploitation of the large area survey, and for some science applications (e.g., studies of
very high redshift) the deep tier will be the most valuable component of the HLWAS. Here, we focus first
on the science trades that govern the large area tiers before turning to a discussion of the deep tier. Our
quantitative discussions focus on the cosmological applications, but our choices of how to resolve these trade
considerations also reflect our assessment of the broader science cases raised in the white papers and our
internal discussions.

4.1

Trade space for the imaging survey

4.1.1

General considerations

At fixed total observing time, the primary trades to be considered in the imaging survey are survey area vs.
exposure time and number of filters. The DRM adopted an imaging survey of 2000 deg2 in Y, J, H, and F184,
with individual exposure times of 140 sec and a scan pattern in which most points on the sky are covered by
5 exposures.
At the time of the DRM, the science team was not confident that weak lensing (WL) measurements could
be made in Y-band, which is the least well sampled by the 0.11-arcsec pixels. The inclusion of F184 in
the DRM was motivated partly by the desire to make WL measurements in 3 bands, allowing cross-checks
among 3 auto-correlations and 3 cross-correlations. F184 imaging also provides additional information for
photometric redshifts (photo-z’s). However, F184 imaging is less sensitive than Y, J, and H because of the
thermal background, and algorithm development by the HLIS PIT (Hirata et al., 2024) and ground measurements of the pixel response have demonstrated that reliable image combination and WL measurements are
feasible with Y-band sampling. The HLIS PIT, therefore, recommended dropping F184 from the large area
tiers, and we have adopted that recommendation, focusing our attention on Y, J, and H.
As discussed more thoroughly in the technical implementation section, the HLIS PIT recommended an
imaging strategy in which there are two time-separated passes at different roll angles, and the imaging in
each filter in each pass has 3 dither positions. Their detailed simulation tests with an implemented image
combination algorithm demonstrate that this 3x2 strategy yields adequate sampling for WL measurements.
They investigated alternative dither/pass strategies that can cover a given sky area in less total observing time,
but they found that these strategies led to worrisomely anisotropic PSF shapes in the reconstructed images.
We have therefore adopted the HLIS PIT’s recommendation of the 3×2 strategy for the large area components
of the HLWAS imaging.
4.1.2

Depth vs. area

Figure 1 shows the total number of WL shapes measured in 100 days of observing time in a given filter,
including the F184 filter and the wide F146 filter, as a function of the exposure time. The wide filter gives the
highest source density, as expected. However, the HLIS PIT judges F146 imaging to be unreliable for WL
because of large chromatic PSF systematics that arise from the difference in SED shapes between stars and
galaxies across the wide filter. We therefore do not consider F146 as an option for the large-area tiers (see
further discussion in Section 4.5). Of the standard medium-width filters, H-band (F158) is the most sensitive
and F184 the least.
The key takeaway from Figure 1 is that the total number of galaxy shape measurements, Ntot = A × neff ,
per unit observing time is nearly constant over a broad range of exposure times, with texp of 91 sec or 149
9

Figure 1: Total number of galaxy shape measurements (left) and total survey area (right) as a function of the
adopted exposure time, for a total observing time of 100 days at ecliptic latitude of 45 deg and the 3 × 2
dither/pass pattern. The WL effective source density neff is computed in each filter for sky locations covered
by five of the six exposures, with a S/N>18 cut assumed for WL sources. In the right panel, the dotted curve
shows a simple Area ∝ 1/texp scaling, while the solid curve includes the impact of slew+settle overheads.
sec giving values just 1.2% lower than the maximum achieved at texp = 116 sec. At shorter texp , Ntot
drops because of the growing impact of overheads and read noise. Forecasts from the HLIS PIT (shown
below) indicate that the cosmological FoM scales approximately with Ntot . Because galaxy number counts
are well approximated by power laws, we expect the total number of galaxy detections above a specified
S/N threshold to follow the same scaling as Ntot , with a different normalization, so optimizing Ntot also
optimizes the statistics for a broad range of extragalactic science cases.
The right panel of Figure 1 shows the area covered per 100 days for a single-filter survey. The divergence
between the solid curve and the dotted A ∝ 1/texp curve shows the impact of overheads. At texp = 91 sec
the impact of overheads is about 19%, so a survey with this exposure time remains efficient in terms of area
covered per unit observing time. At texp = 33 sec the inefficiency due to overheads is a factor of 1.8.
Figure 2 shows other properties of the imaging survey as a function of texp : the 5σ magnitude limit,
the WL effective source density, and the estimated median redshift of the WL source sample. The first
two are smooth functions of texp , with read noise causing the curves to steepen at low texp . The median
redshift exhibits a sharper fall-off at short exposure times. In H-band, the median redshift declines rapidly
for texp < 90 sec and climbs slowly for texp > 90 sec.
4.1.3

Cosmological FoM optimization

The HLIS PIT has carried out a number of cosmological FoM calculations with different assumptions about
the model parameter space. In line with the specifications of the SRD, these calculations focus on constraints
from the cosmic shear power spectrum alone. They include contributions to the error covariance matrix from
shape noise, cosmic variance, and non-Gaussian terms, in particular, the correlation of modes that arises
from non-linear evolution of structure. We caution that the forecasts to date are far from exhaustive, and
the parameter space considered is narrower than what would be used in a full cosmological inference. A
full cosmological inference would also likely include other information from the HLWAS (such as galaxy
clustering and galaxy–galaxy lensing) and from external sources (such as the CMB power spectrum and
BAO and supernova measurements from Roman and external surveys).
Figure 3 shows representative results from the HLIS PIT forecasts conducted in response to a request from
our committee. The key takeaway is that the FoM in each of the cases shown scales linearly with neff and with

10

Figure 2: Image statistics as a function of exposure time. Top left shows the 5σ magnitude limit for point
sources (solid) and exponential-profile sources with a 0.3-arcsec scale length (dotted). Top right shows the
effective WL source density in arcmin−2 . Bottom left shows an estimate of the median redshift of the WL
source population.

11

Figure 3: Scaling of FoM with effective source density, based on forecasts from the HLIS PIT. The left panel
uses the FoM adopted by the SRD, the inverse variance of σ8 considered as a single cosmological parameter.
The right panel allows Ωm to vary as well with a Gaussian prior of 0.01, and the FoM is the inverse covariance
of the two parameters. In both panels, the y-axis scales the FoM by A × neff . Blue and black curves show
survey areas of 4000 deg2 and 7000 deg2 , respectively, and dotted curves show the impact of marginalizing
over uncertainties in baryonic physics, which reduce the cosmological information that could otherwise be
obtained from small angular scales (high l). The nearly flat trend of these curves and the similarity of the blue
and black lines shows that these FoM forecasts scale very closely with Ntot = A × neff .
survey area, to a good but not perfect approximation. Dotted curves show the impact of marginalizing over
one important theoretical systematic, the influence of baryonic physics on the small- scale power spectrum.
Accounting for this systematic lowers the cosmological FoM, as expected, but it does not alter the linear
scaling with area and neff . The HLIS PIT has also computed FoM in a w0 − wa parameter space (describing
the equation-of-state of dark energy instead of assuming a cosmological constant), and the linear scaling
behavior is similar.
The bottom line of this analysis is that optimizing the survey for Ntot = A × neff should be close to
optimizing the WL cosmology performance, and that any choice of texp in the range 91–149 sec is close
to this optimum. We expect this to remain true for analyses that include galaxy–galaxy lensing and galaxy
clustering (known in WL parlance as “3x2-point” analyses), though we do not have detailed forecasts to
demonstrate this point. A choice at the low end of the texp range gives larger survey area, and a choice at the
high end gives higher neff , and there are secondary arguments favoring each of these choices.
Advantages of large area
1. At large angular scales (low l), the dominant source of noise in the power spectrum is cosmic variance, which is independent of neff . If the shape of the power spectrum must be constrained from the
Roman data, improving the precision on large scales will give greater dynamic range for P (k) shape
measurements and thus lead to better cosmological constraints.
2. A shallower survey over wider area concentrates more information on linear scales where theoretical
modeling is easier, and it makes spectroscopic calibration of photo-z’s easier because the sources are
not as faint.
3. A wide area increases overlap with CMB lensing surveys and with cluster catalogs and IGM maps from
X-ray and CMB SZ surveys. It is not clear whether the cosmological return from joint analyses still
scales closely with Ntot or instead favors area relative to neff .
4. For many science applications outside of cosmology, there is a big difference between having some
Roman imaging and no Roman imaging, and much less dependence on the depth of that imaging.
12

5. As a specific instance of #4, having Roman imaging can improve the performance of WL analysis from
Rubin/LSST, for image deblending and for photometric redshifts.
Advantages of high neff
1. For the cosmic shear power spectrum, methods that mitigate the impact of super-sample covariance
(e.g., Digman et al. 2019) may help to recover the n2eff scaling of S/N that applies in the shape-noise
dominated regime. However, the effectiveness of these methods for a Roman-like survey has yet to be
demonstrated.
2. For analyses that use higher-order statistics such as the cosmic shear bispectrum, we expect the scaling
of performance with neff to be steeper than linear. Such analyses may lead to significant sharpening of
cosmological constraints relative to 2-point analyses alone, but this is a field in development and the
likely impact of such analyses is difficult to predict.
3. A deeper survey provides greater precision at higher redshift, and a better leverage on redshift-dependence
of the weak lensing signal may improve constraints for some cosmological models.
4. Achieving high neff and WL precision at high redshift are distinctive capabilities of Roman WL relative
to Rubin and Euclid.
4.1.4

Number of filters

The other critical choice besides texp is the number of filters. A Y+J+H survey would cover one-third the area
of an H-only survey in the same amount of observing time, but it provides better information about galaxy
photo-z’s, provides better control of chromatic PSF biases, allows cross-checks of WL measurements across
different bands, and provides better SED information for studies of galaxy and quasar evolution, Galactic
structure, and stellar astrophysics. In a purely statistical sense, the “loss” in Ntot from doing 3 filters vs. 1 is
less than a factor of three because one can make shape measurements for fainter galaxies. We have estimated
this effect by applying the S/N > 18 cut to the co-added depth of Y+J+H. We find that neff increases by a
factor of 1.4 relative to H-band only, so Ntot in a 3-band survey is lower by a factor 3/1.4 = 2.15 for fixed
total observing time. An actual analysis of a multi-band survey might not use co-added images, but we expect
the statistical impact to be similar. Results for a 2-band survey (J+H) would be intermediate.
Figure 4, provided by the HLIS PIT, shows measures of photo-z performance for the combination of
Rubin LSST photometry with Y+J+H, J+H, or H-only photometry from Roman. These simulation forecasts
apply the calibration techniques currently used in the Dark Energy Survey and planned for LSST. Crucially,
they assume that a complete spectroscopic calibration sample of 20k galaxies to the full depth of the Roman
WL survey will be available in the deep tier, which is probably over-optimistic. Under these assumptions,
more bands improve photo-z performance as expected, and the largest degradation from a J+H or H-only
survey relative to Y+J+H occurs at z > 1.2.
On its own, Figure 4 suggests that even an H-band-only survey, in combination with Rubin photometry,
can yield reasonably good photo-z performance. However, we expect the impact of incompleteness in the
spectroscopic calibration sample to be more severe when the number of Roman bands is smaller. The spectroscopic calibration gives the redshift distribution of galaxies in narrow cells of a high-dimensional color
space with precise photometry in the deep tier. These cells are then mapped to tomographic redshift bins
in the shallower tiers. With fewer filters in the shallow tier, a given tomographic bin contains contributions
from a larger number of calibration cells and is more susceptible to bias from incompleteness in those cells.
There are strategies for measuring and mitigating the impact of imperfect spectroscopic calibration, which
involve using the weak lensing and galaxy clustering data themselves, and cross-correlations with spectroscopic surveys such as DESI and the Roman HLSS, to constrain parameterized offsets in the mean or width
of the redshift distribution in tomographic bins. These strategies are an area of active research.
Unfortunately, we (meaning both our committee and the WL community) are not in a position to give a
confident quantitative assessment of the ultimate impact of photo-z calibration systematics on Roman WL
13

Figure 4: Photo-z performance for Y+J+H, J+H, and H-only Roman surveys in combination with ugrizy
photometry from Rubin at 4-year LSST depth. These plots, provided by the HLIS PIT based on the Cardinal
simulation (To et al., 2024), are based on simulations that assume a complete spectroscopic calibration sample
of 20k galaxies in the deep tier and apply the SOMPZ photo-z calibration framework used in the Dark Energy
Survey. Upper panels show photo-z vs. true redshift. Lower panels show statistical measures of photo-z
quality: the mean absolute deviation, the fraction of outliers, and the bias of the mean photo-z in bins of true
redshift. (Image credit: HLIS PIT, B. Andrews and C. To.)

14

cosmology for different assumptions about the survey depth and filter coverage. This is largely because
we do not know how good the spectroscopic calibration sample will ultimately be and additionally because
mitigation strategies are not yet mature enough to allow accurate forecasts of their performance.

4.2

Trade space for the spectroscopic survey

4.2.1

General considerations

For the spectroscopic survey with a given total exposure time and using a single dispersion element, the
trade space can be characterized as a straightforward (roughly inverse) relationship between depth and area.
The main science cases for the HLWAS spectroscopic survey are based on detecting galaxies through their
emission lines, so the depth can be characterized as a limiting emission line flux. The primary spectroscopic
cosmological measurements (BAOs and RSDs) are based upon correlations of galaxies in redshift space,
so the key consideration is the precise measurement of redshift. The required redshift precision of σz <
0.001(1 + z) requires the grism (with R = 435 − 865) rather than the prism. In the wavelength range
covered by the grism (1.0–1.9 µm), the main emission lines for redshifts are Hα for z = 1–2 and [OIII]
for z = 2–3. As with the imaging survey, longer exposures reach deeper emission-line depths and so larger
source densities, whereas larger areas cover larger numbers of sources.
A further consideration concerns the number of exposures and corresponding roll angles at each position.
The PIT calculations assumed 8 total exposures, two each at 4 different roll angles, which is determined to
be optimal for decontamination of the slitless grism spectra (in that there is improvement with additional roll
angles up to four, but little gain for additional rolls). Our forecasts and the recommended observation plan
follow this strategy. The PIT considered three values for the exposure times for single exposures (assuming
8 total exposures): the DRM value (311.2 s) and 3/4 and 1/2 of that (233.4 s and 155.6 s, referred to as
“descoped DRM” and “DRM-Wide”, respectively).
Another related requirement for the spectroscopic survey is the availability of the imaging observations
needed to perform the spectral decomposition. Studies by the PIT indicate that it is critical to have spacebased, high-resolution near-IR imaging to effectively reduce the grism data, so any spectroscopic field must
be covered by associated Roman imaging. The PIT further specified that imaging in at least two NIR filters is
needed to constrain the spectral slope of sources as part of the decontamination process, using the approach
currently being employed by Euclid. While it may in principle be possible to utilize a combination of one
space-based near-IR image and additional ground-based imaging, this approach would require the development of additional data reduction algorithms, the effectiveness of which are not currently well-established.
The committee therefore has recommended that grism observations be entirely covered by imaging in multiple Roman bands.
An additional parameter has to do with the geometry of the spectroscopic field. The PIT report notes that a
single contiguous field, or a small number of individual fields, are optimal for the cosmological measurements
as they minimize the impact of edge effects and window functions. Our recommended HLWAS spectroscopic
field configuration (Section 5.2) consists of two large contiguous fields, the smaller of which is > 300 deg2
and thus well-suited for the cosmological redshift survey measurements. (For a detailed discussion of a
number of considerations for HLWAS spectroscopy, see Wang et al. 2022.)
4.2.2

Depth vs. area

The predictions provided by the HLSS PIT for the area covered, the number of sources with secure redshifts,
and the comoving space density of sources with redshifts are given in Tables 2, 3, and 4. The areas and total
galaxy numbers are scaled to observations that can be carried out in 100 days of Roman observing time, and
so can be adjusted for larger or smaller allocations of time for spectroscopy3 . It is immediately clear that
the area increases inversely with decreasing exposure time. However, the total number of galaxies in the
3 Please note that the calculations used for the PIT forecasts presented in Tables 2–5 differ somewhat from those used to produce the
values given in Table 1; for details of our calculations using the Roman ETC see Section 5.

15

Table 2: Area covered by an HLWAS spectroscopic survey per 100 days of observing time, for different
values of the grism exposure time. Credit: HLSS PIT, Y. Wang.

Table 3: Number of secure redshifts in two redshift bins (z = 1–2 and z = 2–3) per 100 days of Roman
observing time, for different values of the exposure time. Credit: HLSS PIT, Y. Wang.
various redshift bins is mostly insensitive to the choice of exposure time, as the lower density of sources at
lower flux limits is approximately balanced by the larger area. For science cases that depend primarily on the
total number of galaxies (like some of the WL measurements described in the previous section), the science
return is only weakly dependent on exposure time. However, for measurements that depend on additional
parameters such as the populations of galaxies being probed, the density of galaxies, or the total number of
pairs at particular scales, the precision can depend strongly on the trade-off between exposure time and area.
4.2.3

Cosmological FoM optimization

The Roman HLSS PIT provided forecasts of the FoMs for the main BAO and RSD cosmological measurements, as defined in the SRD and as discussed in Section 3.1. These FoMs are scaled to 100 days of observing
time, but scale approximately linearly with larger or smaller time allocations. These FoM estimates clearly
show that shorter exposure times, and correspondingly greater area, are valuable in maximizing both the BAO
and RSD FoMs.
For statistical precision of the cosmological measurements, there is therefore an incentive to increase
the area as much as possible. However, the total possible area is constrained by several factors, including
the availability of multi-band Roman imaging and the potential systematics introduced by lower signal-tonoise of the shallower spectroscopic measurements. There is also some dependence with exposure time
on constraints on the redshift evolution of cosmological parameters, with deeper observations giving better
constraints at high z (Figure 5). A number of general astrophysics science cases benefit from deeper spectroscopic observations to perform detailed emission line analysis, gain access to fainter emission lines, and
probe less luminous galaxy populations.
We note that the absolute values of the FoMs for the RSD measurements provided by the PIT are somewhat lower than the corresponding forecasts presented in the SRD for comparable survey areas and numbers
of redshifts. This difference is due to differing assumptions in the forecasts for the RSD analysis, but the scaling with survey properties should still be similar. The formal science requirements in the SRD are given in
terms of the FoMs that would be achievable from the DRM, with a specified margin, as described in Section
2.1. Our recommended spectroscopic observations satisfy the mission requirements, as discussed in Section
4.4.

17

4.3

The deep calibration tier

Our recommendations for the deep tier are based primarily on the data requirements for calibration specified
by the HLIS and HLSS PITs. However, we expect that the deep-tier data will prove enormously powerful for
a wide range of science, reaching depths fainter than the famous CANDELS (Grogin et al., 2011; Koekemoer
et al., 2011) and 3D-HST (Brammer et al., 2012) surveys over an area ∼ 100 times larger.
The priorities specified by the HLIS PIT for deep-tier imaging are to reach at least 10x the effective
exposure time of the medium tier (increasing the limiting magnitude by 1.25 mag) over 20 deg2 and to reach
at least 30x the effective exposure time (+1.85 mag) over part of this area. Effective exposure time is defined
in an inverse variance sense and includes the impact of ecliptic latitude of the deep-tier fields relative to the
median of the medium tier. The 10x depth imaging will be used for photo-z calibration, so it should be carried
out in many bands and in fields that have deep optical imaging and are accessible to powerful spectroscopic
resources. In practice, the spectroscopic facilities for which we can be confident of access to the Roman
community are Subaru PFS (Tamura et al., 2016), DESI (DESI Collaboration et al., 2016), and Keck4 , all in
the northern hemisphere. The 30x depth imaging will be used to test for noise biases on weak lensing shape
measurements, so it is needed only in the Y, J, H bands used for the medium tier.
The priority specified by the HLSS PIT for the deep tier is to obtain spectroscopy with 10 passes, each
equivalent to one pass of the regular spectroscopic survey (which has 2 passes at different roll angles), over an
area of 22 deg2 . These observations should have a range of roll angles, ideally sampling the full 360 degrees,
but at least covering a range around the medium-tier roll angles.
Our recommendations for the deep tier (see Section 4.4) meet these priorities, though the restriction to
fields that can be observed from the northern hemisphere means that we cannot access all roll angles for the
spectroscopy. For many of the science cases presented in white papers, the deep tier will be the single most
valuable component of the HLWAS. This is one reason we include the Z087, F184, and K213 bands in our
recommended 10x imaging; they will be useful for many science cases as well as for improving photo-z
calibration. Although many white papers propose science that will benefit from the deep-tier data, none of
them make a strong case for an area larger than 20 deg2 .

4.4

Recommended in-guide survey design and rationale

We describe the in-guide recommendation for each of the three survey tiers, in turn, with a brief summary
of the rationale drawing on the discussion in Sections 4.1–4.3. We describe details of our recommended
implementation, including field choice, scanning patterns, and scheduling considerations, in Section 5, which
also contains our overguide and underguide recommendations. A summary is in the table given above in the
Executive Summary. The medium and wide tier areas in Table 1 are slightly larger than the round numbers we
give in the text below, because our observing time estimate for the deep tier is slightly shorter than the 70 days
we had initially set aside in our calculations. The areas in the summary table constitute our recommendation,
but these small differences are within the uncertainties of exact time requirements for the survey observing
modes and scheduling constraints.
4.4.1

Big Picture

Relative to the DRM, the key change is the addition of a wide tier of H-band only imaging, enabled by
reduced exposure times and filter coverage in the medium tier. Even in a single band, near-IR imaging at
Roman resolution is valuable for many science cases, especially in combination with Rubin ground-based
imaging. For weak lensing, the addition of the wide tier significantly increases the expected FoM. We elected
to keep the ratio of imaging:spectroscopy in the medium+wide tiers at 2:1, similar to that in the DRM and
earlier studies for WFIRST-AFTA (Spergel et al., 2015). Trade studies shows that the GRS FoM increases
with shorter exposures and larger area at fixed total observing time, and we have moved in this direction
relative to the DRM. However, robust implementation of the GRS also requires Roman imaging in at least
4 https://www2.keck.hawaii.edu/

18

two filters, so it is restricted to the medium tier. We have balanced these considerations by moderately
increasing the area of the medium tier (to 2400 deg2 ) and choosing an exposure time that allows this area to
be covered in approximately 150 days of grism observations. The resulting FoM is somewhat lower than that
of the DRM, which reflects the fact that the 520-day in-guide recommendation for the HLWAS is lower than
the approximately 2 years envisioned for the DRM.
4.4.2

Deep Tier

As the choice of fields for the deep tier, we recommend the two Rubin/ LSST Deep Drilling Fields that are
closest to the equator, COSMOS and XMM-LSS, each of them 9.6 deg2 in area. The equatorial location
makes the fields accessible to both northern and southern hemisphere telescopes, the deep LSST imaging
will provide the optical counterpart to the deep Roman imaging and spectroscopy, and the total area is close
to that requested by the HLIS and HLSS PITs. The ecliptic latitudes of these fields, centered at −9.4 deg
and −17.9 deg, respectively, require additional exposure time to reach the 10x effective exposure time of the
medium tier, but this cost seems worth the benefits.
Per the HLIS PIT request, we recommend a combination of dithers, passes, and exposure time sufficient
to reach a depth equivalent to 10x exposure time at the median ecliptic latitude of the medium tier, over the
full area of the two deep drilling fields in Y, J, and H, and the same total exposure time in Z087, F184, K213,
and W146. We recommend covering 5 deg2 of the COSMOS field to the deeper 30x level in Y, J, and H
only. We recommend including W146 in the deep tier so that the community can test ideas for surveys that
might use only W146, as proposed in some white papers and published articles. The W146 component of the
deep tier may be especially valuable for solar system science, where detection of objects that move between
individual exposures requires the highest sensitivity.
Per the HLSS PIT request, we recommend observing the full area of both deep drilling fields with grism
observations that are individually matched to the dither pattern and exposure time of the medium tier while
covering a wider range of roll angles, with a total depth equivalent to 10x the medium tier exposure time at
its median ecliptic latitude.
With the implementation described in Section 5, we estimate that the deep tier will require a total of 55
days of observing time.
4.4.3

Medium Tier

For the medium tier, we recommend a survey area of 2400 deg2 in Y, J, and H imaging bands and grism
spectroscopy. For the imaging, we recommend a strategy of two passes at distinct roll angles, with 3 dither
positions per filter in each pass, and an exposure time of 107.25 sec. Details are given in Section 5. For the
spectroscopy we recommend a strategy of four passes at distinct roll angles, with two dither positions in each
pass and an exposure time of 189.75 sec.
For both imaging and spectroscopy, this medium tier is similar to the originally envisioned, 2000 deg2
DRM, but the imaging is somewhat shallower and no longer includes F184, the spectroscopy is shallower,
and the area is larger. (DRM exposure times were 140 sec in imaging and 311 sec in spectroscopy.)
For the imaging survey, we estimate neff = 41.3 arcmin−2 at the median ecliptic latitude and a total
number of weak lensing shape measurements Ntot = 3.6 × 108 . This is nearly identical to the predicted Ntot
for the DRM observing strategy. Since our FoM forecasts (from the HLIS PIT) scale with this total number
of shape measurements, this implies that the medium tier alone has a WL statistical power comparable to the
estimate for the DRM, even before the addition of the wide tier.
For the spectroscopic survey, we estimate a total of 14.2M Hα redshifts, 3.6M [O III] redshifts, and 1.3M
[O II] redshifts. Of these, 11M are at z > 1 and 1.7M are at z > 2. (To account for the adopted texp and
effects such as chip gaps and ecliptic latitude variations, we carried out these estimates separately from the
HLSS PIT, using the Roman ETC. Because the assumptions are different, our estimated yields are somewhat
different from those provided by the HLSS PIT.) For FoM estimates, we interpolated the calculations from
the HLSS PIT (see Table 5). We find that the medium tier GRS would give a BAO FoM about 0.83 times

19

that of the DRM (assuming 2000 deg2 and 311.2 sec exposures for the latter), with a similar ratio for RSD.
This is close to the SRD requirement of 0.85. (Recall that the SRD specifies that the FoMs should exceed the
DRM FoM divided by 1.5, with a factor of 0.85 of that margin attributed to survey area.)
We estimate that the medium tier will require 212 days of imaging and 158 days of spectroscopy, so 370
days total.
We seriously considered adopting shorter exposures of 91 sec for imaging and 155 sec for spectroscopy,
which would have allowed the medium tier to cover more area in the same amount of observing time. We
opted for the longer exposures of 107 sec and 190 sec to stay safely away from rapid falloffs in the galaxy
number densities and to lean towards the high neff and high spectroscopic number density that are distinctive
features of Roman relative to Euclid. Furthermore, while area would be relatively easy to add in an extended
mission, the depth difference between these exposure times would not (in particular, time lost to overheads
cannot be recovered), so it seems prudent to err on the side of depth. Greater depth is also valuable for galaxy
evolution studies at higher redshift, which is another distinctive strength of Roman.
4.4.4

Wide Tier

For the wide tier, we recommend applying all time remaining after accounting for the deep and medium tiers
to an H-band only survey, with the same 107 s exposure time used for H-band in the medium tier. With 70
days for the deep tier and 370 days for the medium tier, the in-guide allocation of 520 days for the HLWAS
leaves 80 days for the wide tier, sufficient to survey 2700 deg2 .
For WL, the predicted effective surface density is neff = 26.7 arcmin−2 at the median ecliptic latitude,
comparable to that anticipated for Euclid and LSST. The total number of shape measurements is 2700 ×
3600 × 26.7 = 2.6 × 108 vs. 3.6 × 108 for the medium tier. Thus, with about 25% of the imaging time, the
wide tier can nearly double the number of WL shape measurements, which should increase the FoM by the
same factor if the cosmological interpretation remains statistics limited. There is greater risk that the wide
tier WL will be limited by systematics, but we are optimistic that these can be adequately controlled given
guidance from the deep and medium tiers.
The 2700 deg2 of wide tier imaging will be valuable for many science applications, especially those that
exploit overlap with other surveys (such as LSST, CMB experiments, and eROSITA) and Galactic studies,
where structure is not statistically homogeneous across the sky. In addition to the value on its own, we view
the wide tier as a “down payment” towards a much larger area H-band survey that could be undertaken in an
extended mission if the science arguments for more sky coverage are strong enough (see Section 8).

4.5

Roads not taken

The clearest case of a “road not taken” in our recommended implementation of the HLWAS is a wide area
tier using the W146 filter, which can reach roughly 0.8 mag deeper and neff ∼ 1.6× higher in the same texp
relative to H158 (see Figures 1 and 2). Thus, with W146 we could reduce texp and cover wider area. One
ambitious white paper proposed an aggressive scan strategy that would cover sky area very rapidly to a depth
mAB ∼ 25 in W146 but with undersampled images.
We decided at the outset that all HLWAS imaging should follow a scan strategy that allows image reconstruction adequate for WL shape measurements. While some astrophysics can be pursued with undersampled
images, analysis is harder in the best of cases and nearly impossible in others. For a data set as large and with
as broad uses as the HLWAS, we regard recovery of fully sampled imaging as a requirement.
Our primary reason for choosing H158 rather than W146 for the wide tier imaging is the concern raised
by the HLIS PIT that W146 imaging would not be usable for WL analysis because of chromatic PSF bias
that arises from difference in SED shape between stars and galaxies. This effect scales roughly as the square
of the filter width. Figure 6, provided by the HLIS PIT, shows that predicted multiplicative bias in shear
measurement for the W146 band is about 2%. This is 100 times larger than the shear calibration requirement
specified in the SRD (dark gray band), and 20 times larger than even a relaxed requirement (light gray band)
that allows for self-calibration of shear bias from the WL correlation measurements themselves. Colored

20

lines show some possible mitigation strategies, and while it is conceivable that these would advance to the
required level by the time the survey is available, the simulations that produce these predictions are themselves
simplified in the assumed range of SED shapes. Relying on undeveloped mitigation strategies to achieve a
factor of 20–100 improvement relative to the “raw” chromatic PSF bias seems too risky.
Chromatic PSF bias is also an issue for H158 imaging only, as we will not have galaxy-by-galaxy information about the near-IR SED shape. However, the raw effect is 10 times smaller in the narrower filter, and it
seems likely that mitigation strategies can bring it to an acceptable level. Chromatic PSF bias remains a risk
for the H-band only wide tier, but we regard this risk as small.
While the W146 filter reaches greater depth in a given exposure time, it is a broad, non-standard filter, and
it provides less SED-shape discrimination when combined with LSST photometry. For some applications, the
gain in sensitivity with W146 may be outweighed by the reduced SED information. The inclusion of W146
imaging in the deep tier will allow testing of W146-only strategies for many science cases, including WL
cosmology. Conceivably, by the time the wide tier imaging begins, these tests will have established W146
as a robust and better alternative to H158. However, we strongly recommend H158 rather than W146 as the
baseline.
Another “road not taken” is to reduce or even eliminate the medium tier, 3-band survey in favor of maximizing the survey area in H158, a strategy advocated in some white papers. In the absence of systematics, an
H-band only survey would yield higher Ntot and higher WL FoM than the 3-band survey. However, we regard the control and testing of WL systematics and the greater astrophysical information on detected objects
to be hallmark strengths of the HLWAS, especially as a complement to Euclid. We therefore elected to retain
a medium tier comparable to the original 2000 deg2 DRM. Furthermore, the spectroscopic survey requires
at least two bands for robust discrimination of possible contaminants. Allowing some of the survey area to
be J+H+grism rather than Y+J+H+grism would have increased the time available for the wide tier, but we
decided that the greater complexity of having three “layers” of survey in addition to the deep tier would not
be worth a fairly modest gain in area.
A final “road not taken” is to eliminate the wide tier, which would allow the medium tier to expand by
about 500 deg2 , to 2900 deg2 total. While a uniform strategy would simplify analysis, we regard the gains of
spending 80 days on the wide tier — more than doubling the area coverage, and nearly doubling the galaxy
numbers — to be worth the greater complexity.

5

Technical implementation of the Proposed Survey

The Nominal survey consists of several layers. The Medium Tier is most similar to the DRM design and
includes 3 bands of NIR imaging (Y+J+H) as well as grism spectroscopy. The Wide Tier is an extension of
the imaging in H band (at the same exposure time) only to provide space-resolution NIR imaging and weak
lensing shapes over a larger area. The Deep Tier observations are carried out in 2 equatorial calibration fields
(COSMOS and XMM-LSS): these are selected to be LSST deep drilling fields, as well as accessible from
Subaru and other northern observatories, and have additional filters (spanning Z through K) as well as deeper
Y+J+H+Grism coverage. A subset of the COSMOS area forms an Ultra Deep Tier, needed for calibrating
blending effects in weak lensing investigations.
The scripts used to generate the coverage maps and tilings in this section can be found at
https://github.com/hirata10/RomanHLWASTools/tree/v0.1.

5.1

Tiling

In the imaging filters, the sky is tiled in two passes, with 3 dither positions (after each observation, the field
of view is moved “up” (WFI +Y direction, see Fig. 7) by ≈ 1/3 of a field, and dithered diagonally to move
the chip gaps). [The ≈ 1/3 field step is a change from the original Reference strategy, which did 3 small
dithers followed by a full field step. This was done to provide a more uniform survey since the “bottom”
row of SCAs has higher read noise than the others.] This leads to a set of “strips” along the sky, which can

21

Figure 6: Multiplicative bias in shear calibration caused by the difference in PSF shape between stars and
galaxies across the filter band, for W146 (top) and H158 (bottom). Black curves show the uncorrected
bias for galaxies at different redshifts. Colored curves show the bias after mitigation strategies that correct
based on average galaxy SEDs in redshift bins. Dark gray bands show the systematic error budget for shape
measurements from the SRD, and light gray bands show a relaxed requirement that may be adequate if the
shear measurement bias can be calibrated internally from the data, e.g., using the scaling of WL amplitude
with redshift. (Image Credit: HLIS PIT, F. Berlfein & R. Mandelbaum.)

22

Figure 7: Schematic of the “strip” concept used in the sky tiling within each pass. The first panel indicates
the Wide Field Instrument coordinate system and chip numbering; the +Y direction is “up”.
then be horizontally joined to cover a patch of sky. The two passes are rolled 167◦ relative to each other:
being approximately 180◦ apart allows the second pass to be scheduled 4–6 months later (when the Sun is in
the opposite direction relative to the field). The additional 13◦ roll prevents large-scale Moiré patterns from
the two passes, and improves internal calibration by allowing repeat observations of objects across any pair
of SCAs, and will enable de-striping of 1/f noise. It also guarantees that the diffraction spikes from bright
stars are “interlaced”, so that an object impacted by a far diffraction spike in one pass falls in between the
diffraction spikes in the other pass. (There are 12 diffraction spikes, with spacings of alternately ∼ 25◦ and
35◦ .)
The spectroscopic survey uses 4 passes, each paired to give opposite orientations. These have only 2
dither positions in each pass (again stepped by ≈ 1/2 field, and with a small horizontal dither to move the
chip gaps).
The deep fields have a longer imaging exposure time (295 sec), since there is no reason to accept the read
noise and overhead penalties of the Medium/Wide exposure time. Both are near the Ecliptic where the roll
angles available are limited, half of the passes are rolled in a range of angles around the “North is up” direction
and the other half are in a range around the “South is up” orientation. We have confirmed with the HLSS
PIT that this range of available roll angles will be sufficient for the spectroscopic calibration. The nominal
plan is for 5 passes in the standard imaging filters (ZYJHFK); 6 passes in the Wide filter (both to go deeper
and to provide in-flight data on shape and astrometry systematics that could inform future observations); and
36 passes in the grism (but keeping the 190 s exposures). An “ultra-deep” region of 5 deg2 in the COSMOS
field has an additional 10 passes in Y, J, and H, to support blending calibration for the weak lensing analysis.
Examples of medium and deep coverage maps are shown in Figure 8.
The cosmology objectives are agnostic to the precise timing of the deep field observations (although it
is important to get to >2x medium tier depth in these fields as early as possible to test out the pipeline and
identify anything that might need to be adjusted; and we restrict the observations to be >75° from the Sun,
with a preference for farther). However, the timing of the deep field observations will have a significant
impact on their usability for Solar System investigations. (The COSMOS field in particular is only 9° from
the Ecliptic.) The preference is for at least 2 pairs of visits, each 9–24 hours apart, with a spacing of order
∼ 1 month. Given the typically ≈ 9 hours required to do a pass over a Deep Field, and the plan for a total
of 6 passes in the Wide filter and 20 in the standard-width Z/Y/J/H filters (which are ∼ 0.6 mag shallower),
23

Figure 8: Examples of coverage maps from a tiling simulation. Left: an example from the medium tier
imaging, showing the two rolled passes. Middle: an example from the medium tier spectroscopy, with two
pairs of opposite rolls; this has been zoomed out to show the dislocations due to curved sky tiling effects.
Right: the COSMOS deep field (in this case note that the deep observations are 2.8x longer than the wide
observations, so the deep tier is deeper than the observation count would suggest). The survey edge effects
are visible in this case.
there are many internally consistent solutions for such strategies.
Roman will be uniquely sensitive to more distant solar system objects, which are particularly challenging
from the ground due to their slower rates of motion. With two visits approximately 10 hours apart, objects
at 565 au would move 1 pixel and objects at 120 au would move approximately 10 pixels. An ideal cadence
would be to acquire two passes with a 9–24 hour separation and 4–6 weeks later acquire a second pair
separated by 9–24 hours. A third pair acquired 4–8 weeks later (including only the Wide filter in the second
set) could be successfully linked to previous detections. (Over 4 weeks, an object at 565 au will move
approximately 7 arcseconds and at 120 au will move 1.2 arcminutes, so the shear off the pointings over 8–12
weeks will not be significant.) Note that the detection rate limit will likely be set by the closest time separation
of two sets. The discovery detection limit will be set by the Wide filter, but brighter objects will be detected
in multiple bands.

5.2

Footprint Locations

The selected footprint locations are a compromise among several competing considerations. Critical to the
HLWAS are to be (i) far away from the Galactic plane to minimize dust and crowding; and (ii) have optical
band coverage from LSST. Another set of considerations, pushing in opposite directions, are (i) being far
from the Ecliptic (where the sky brightness is greatest, especially for the Medium tier); and (ii) accessibility
from Northern Hemisphere facilities, e.g., at Mauna Kea and Kitt Peak (this is critical for the deep calibration
fields, but for the medium/wide tiers partial coverage is acceptable). A third set of considerations include
overlap with other existing/planned extragalactic deep fields and wide surveys.
This section describes the notional In-Guide footprint that the committee considered, which meets the
critical considerations while achieving compromises on the others. The survey footprint is displayed in Figure 9, and the histograms of its properties are displayed in Figure 10. The distribution of optimal scheduling
windows for this footprint is displayed in Figure 11.
Most of the Wide tier ends up near the Ecliptic, because the regions visible from LSST and Northern
Hemisphere facilities and far from the Ecliptic and Galactic planes are limited, and these regions were priori24

The Medium tier (Yellow) and Wide tier (Red) are shown, superposed on the SFD dust map (the Galactic
Plane is visible in purple). The Deep calibration fields COSMOS and XMM-LSS are filled in dark gray.
Several other LSST and Euclid deep southern fields (outlined) are included in the Medium tier.
