because of read noise/overheads, and also because many imaging science cases scale less strongly with depth
than the emission line galaxy luminosity function).
The great majority of the footprint (87%) is at low reddening, E(B − V ) < 0.05 (Schlegel et al., 1998),
and almost all (99.6%) is at E(B − V ) < 0.1. It is not practical to truly avoid all regions of higher reddening
due to the filamentary structure of the Galactic dust.

5.3

Underguide and Overguide Recommendations

After considering a number of possibilities, we have concluded that in the underguide or overguide scenario,
we would adjust the area of the wide tier while leaving the deep tier and medium tier unchanged. The survey
speed in the wide tier is 33.94 deg2 /day, so the underguide allocation of 480 days would decrease the wide
tier area by 33.94 × 40 = 1358 deg2 , while the overguide allocation of 540 days would increase the wide tier
area by 33.94 × 20 = 679 deg2 . In the underguide scenario, the area of the wide tier would be 1344 deg2 , and
the total area of the medium+wide tiers would be 3759 deg2 . In the overguide scenario, the wide tier would
be 3381 deg2 , and medium+wide would be 5796 deg2 .
We adopted this approach to the underguide and overguide scenarios for several reasons. First and foremost, we regard the deep and medium tiers as the “core mission” of the HLWAS, providing detailed data for
many science investigations, tight control of weak lensing systematics, and spectroscopy as well as imaging;
the wide tier exploits the opportunity afforded by efficient survey strategies and telescope performance to
gain sky coverage and statistical power for cosmology. Second, the constraining power of the spectroscopic
survey is already slightly reduced relative to the DRM, and we do not wish to reduce it further. Third, we
hope that the wide tier will eventually be expanded to larger area in an extended mission, in which case the
underguide/overguide scenarios amount to a trade between prime and extended mission time. We think it
27

less likely (though not impossible) that an extended mission would be used to expand the more expensive,
YJH+grism observations of the medium tier.
For most science cases, the return from the wide tier varies smoothly with survey area. However, with
a 480-day allocation the “wide tier” is substantially smaller in area than the medium tier, so its stand-alone
value for most applications is limited. We strongly advocate keeping the wide tier at least the same area
as the medium tier, which requires a minimum allocation of 510 days; even this mild underguide moves
the total survey area below the symbolic threshold of 5000 deg2 . Conversely, the overguide scenario moves
the wide tier alone above the symbolic threshold of 3000 deg2 and makes the total number of galaxy shape
measurements nearly equal in the two tiers. Thus, the overguide tells a significantly nicer story.
We reiterate that the wide tier substantially expands the opportunities for synergy with other surveys
including LSST, eROSITA, and CMB experiments in the southern hemisphere. Our placement of the wide
tier fields balances considerations of accessibility to northern hemisphere telescopes against overlap with
southern hemisphere surveys. Changes of a few hundred deg2 in the wide tier can change the available overlap
with those surveys (see Table 6) by a large factor. In particular, the overguide would significantly increase
the overlap with the surveys conducted by the South Pole Telescope (SPT), most notably the 1500 deg2 SPT3G-Main survey (the overlap with SPT-3G-Main increases from 22 deg2 in the in-guide plan — basically a
triangle around the ELAIS field5 — up to 473 deg2 in the overguide), which is likely to remain the deepest
wide-area CMB survey over the duration of the Roman mission. Overlap with CMB experiments enables a
number of scientific studies, from cosmology with cluster counts, to CMB × lensing cross-correlation studies,
baryonic feedback studies, and more. The SPT fields are well characterized, and in particular, the SPT cluster
count cosmology with weak-lensing mass calibration is in a mature state. Expanding overlap with SPT is one
of the most valuable opportunities in the overguide scenario.
We will provide a detailed footprint choice for the underguide or overguide scenario (or an intermediate
case) if the ROTAC chooses to recommend one or the other. The wide tier includes area in the northern and
southern Galactic caps. In the underguide scenario, we would take area from both of these regions, as each of
them has distinct value (accessibility to northern hemisphere telescopes vs. overlap with southern hemisphere
surveys). In the overguide scenario, we would add area to the southern cap region, since the northern cap
footprint already covers most of the area that has both low extinction and LSST coverage.

6

Constraints and Considerations

The principal cosmology goals of the HLWAS could be met by many variants of the field selection and are
relatively insensitive to scheduling details. However, there are some constraints and considerations that will
affect the detailed design and scheduling of the survey. We summarize these in this section.
Scheduling constraints: While there are no hard constraints on the ordering of observations, there are orderings that are preferable for operational reasons, to enhance particular science cases, or to ensure some science
analyses are possible earlier in the mission:
• Early deep fields: At least 2 passes in each filter (ZYJHFKW), and at least 8 passes in the grism (4
leading and 4 trailing), should be accomplished very early in the mission to confirm that the survey
strategy and data reduction work as expected and inform any adjustments necessary. This should be
prior to the start of the other surveys for the highest-priority data (Y, J, and H band passes, and 4 of the
grism passes, either those dispersed North or those dispersed South depending on field availability);
and within the first year of the mission for the remainder.
• Ultra Deep Tier: The Ultra Deep layer is needed for downstream shear analysis, and we recommend
reaching at least half the depth in the first 2 years of the mission.
• Medium and Wide tiers: There is a preference to schedule Medium tier observations mostly — but
not entirely — before the Wide tier when possible, because we have the greatest confidence in our
5 The

SPT-3G-Main footprint does not extend far enough East to cover the Euclid Deep Field South.

28

understanding of systematic effects in the Medium tier and plan to use this to inform the analysis of
the Wide tier. Within the Medium tier, the preference is to obtain at least 1 pass of imaging data in Y,
J, and H prior to the grism passes (although the acquisition of the grism data does not depend on the
imaging data, the grism spectra cannot be processed and scientifically utilized until the imaging data
are available). The request is not to schedule all of the Medium tier first and then the Wide tier because
of the need for interlacing (see below).
• Interlacing: The cross-calibration of the Northern and Southern Galactic caps has been a challenge in
previous large-scale structure surveys. There are seasons where both are accessible from L2 (e.g., in
December the Northern Galactic cap region is visible in the “leading” direction in Earth’s orbit, while
the Southern Galactic cap region is visible in the “trailing” direction). We recommend that during these
periods observations in the two caps are interlaced (e.g., a few days of one and then a few days of the
other) so that slow zero-point drifts can be distinguished from true offsets in the photometry in the two
caps. Similarly, some strips of Wide survey should be interlaced with Medium. This internal calibration
is in addition to (but does not replace) the planned tracking of zero-points via the Touchstone fields.
• Solar system object searches: There is a preference to schedule the COSMOS field observations in
pairs of passes 9–24 hours apart, with a second pair 4–6 weeks later (see Section 5.1). While the Wide
filter is optimal for detection, additional epochs may be obtained in the other filters.
Location of specific fields: The Medium/Wide survey footprint was chosen by the committee on the basis
of overlap with other facilities, sky brightness, and Galactic foregrounds. Modest changes to the footprint
(e.g., moving the edges a few degrees here and there) would be acceptable, provided that the Deep fields
are still included. We also deliberately included the other LSST Deep drilling fields (CDFS and ELAIS)
and the Euclid Deep Field-South in the Medium footprint; while they are not Roman HLWAS deep fields,
many synergies are possible with the Roman Medium data, and we want to enable tying these fields to the
Medium survey if they are covered in the GAS program (as seems likely given the interest from the white
papers). Since the Roman HLWAS footprint will likely factor into observing proposals on other facilities, it
is important that once we have iterated with the scheduling group and converged to a final footprint, that this
choice be publicly communicated and that there be a high bar for any further modifications.
The Deep tier choices of COSMOS and XMM-LSS are the only fields with both LSST deep drilling
and good accessibility from Northern Hemisphere facilities. The committee does not see any reasonable
alternatives to these fields.
The relative roll angles of the passes interact with scheduling due to the orientation constraint relative to
the Sun (instantaneously ±15◦ , but except for fields on the Ecliptic the direction to the Sun varies slowly).
The imaging passes were arranged with a 167◦ relative roll to provide for internal calibration and move the
diffraction spikes; but varying this ±5◦ either way would also satisfy these needs. It is also possible to
flip an imaging pass upside-down (so 13◦ relative roll) instead (e.g., two “leading season” or two “trailing
season” passes instead of one of each), although with the asymmetric nature of the Roman field of view one
needs to be careful about efficiency losses at the edges if this is done often. For the grism, the balance of 2
leading season and 2 trailing season passes, with nearly opposed dispersion direction, is important to break
the degeneracy between the location of the centroid of the line-emitting region and the wavelength (hence
redshift) of the source galaxy.
Sensitivity to start and end of science operations: Because the HLWAS survey constraints repeat every
year, the principal impact would be from which month of the year science operations begin. In particular, the
early deep field that one would observe changes depending on when in the year we are ready to observe it:
COSMOS is best positioned in late April–May or November/December; and XMM-LSS is best positioned
in January or August. The spacing of the two deep fields guarantees that at least one could be covered in the
first ∼4 months of science operations.
Ancillary data: The principal ancillary data sets required from other observatories are optical photometry
(from LSST) and calibration spectroscopy in the deep fields (which will include a substantial contribution
29

from Subaru/PFS). Other overlapping large-area surveys (including those with multiwavelength data ranging
from radio to X-ray wavelengths) will enhance the HLWAS science, and we have taken these into account in
our footprint design.
We note that the HLIS PIT request for blending calibration fields included two ultra-deep fields of 5 deg2
each, requiring 6.4 days each to provide the extra depth beyond the Deep tier. The proposed tiling includes one
5 deg2 field, centered at COSMOS. The southern HLTDS field, if it follows previous notional plans (Akeson
et al., 2019), would provide a second field (it does not need to coincide with an HLWAS deep field or be
accessible from Northern spectroscopic instruments for blending calibration). If the selected implementation
of the HLTDS does not provide YJH coverage meeting this need for the second field, then consideration
should be given to adding a second ultra deep field in the HLWAS, with a corresponding reduction in the time
allocated to the wider areas.
Pre-survey requirements: In addition to “standard” commissioning activities (e.g., validating throughput
and overhead models), the early Deep field observations are needed to validate the survey depths and galaxy
yields before committing to the full wide-area survey. Note that the requested Deep field observations for this
purpose are only a subset of the total Deep field observations in the HLWAS.

7

Technical checkpoints from early survey data analysis

The most stringent performance requirements for the HLWAS derive from cosmology mission goals. The
Medium and Wide tiers cover large areas to provide statistical power for cosmology and general astrophysics,
while the Deep tier is used to calibrate the Medium and Wide tier measurements, and measure faint objects
with many-band photometry and grism spectroscopy. The Medium tier most closely resembles the original
DRM, designed to provide the highest level of systematics control of contemporary major weak lensing
projects (Euclid, LSST, Roman), and optimized for a high FoM in terms of σ8 (see Section 3.1). The Wide
tier is complementary, being optimized to cover the largest possible area for general astrophysics, while still
maintaining sufficient control of systematic uncertainties to support weak lensing shear measurements for a
broad range of cosmological goals which scale mostly with survey area (e.g., dark energy; Eifler et al., 2021,
2024).
Accordingly, on-orbit characterization of delivered data quality and calibration methods, including combinations of Roman data with LSST for photo-z estimation, will be important to validate both the Medium
and Wide tier designs. Furthermore, the Wide tier is envisioned as an initial investment towards even largerarea surveys that could be pursued with GAS programs and/or in an extended mission, and the design should
also be evaluated in this context.
In Section 6, we recommend that the HLWAS begin with an initial set of Deep tier observations, in
part, to enable early validation of the Medium and Wide tier designs. The longer exposure times, larger
number of dithered pointings, broader multi-band coverage, and overlap with external datasets for the Deep
tier will provide direct, high signal-to-noise, on-orbit measurements of the flux distribution of galaxies in
the Roman passbands, and allow tests to calibrate images, perform source measurements, and extract spectra
for fainter objects than the limiting sensitivity of the Medium and Wide tiers. The committee recommends
that the Medium and Wide tiers use the same exposure time and tiling pattern to provide uniform H-band
coverage over the full HLWAS footprint. The early Deep tier observations should be used to validate the
Deep, Medium, and Wide tier strategy as a comprehensive survey program, in particular, considering that
cosmology analyses will likely combine measurements from the three tiers.
We further recommend that the Medium tier observations be (mostly) scheduled before the Wide tier
observations to provide an additional layer of survey strategy validation and potential re-optimization. The
subset of Medium tier H-band observations are effectively a pilot for the Wide tier since the two tiers use
an identical tiling pattern. Thus, the Medium tier observations can be used to directly evaluate the Wide tier
design, and compare object measurements for the single H band versus JHY combination.
We emphasize that re-optimization of the survey strategy after launch should have a high bar, driven by
substantially new technical understanding that emerges from on-orbit characterization, that would substan30

tially impact the broad science portfolio of the Roman mission. For reasons discussed below, most of the
survey strategy re-optimization options are more likely to be relevant for the Wide rather than the Medium
tier. Critical technical parameters that could potentially impact the Medium and Wide tier survey strategy
include:
• SNR limit and effective number density for weak lensing source galaxies. The recommended design is based on a SNR>18 cut for weak-lensing galaxies. The Deep tier data should be utilized to
verify this limit.
– If a higher SNR limit is found to be necessary, the exposure time for the Medium / Wide tier
might need to be increased, leading to a reduction of the survey area.
– If a lower SNR limit is deemed acceptable, the exposure time could be decreased in order to
capture more area.
• PSF modeling and weak lensing shear measurement. Deep tier observations should be used to verify
that the planned number of dithers, passes, and roll angle combinations for the Medium and Wide tiers
enable the required precision for shear estimates.
– If the recommended tiling pattern is found to be insufficient for weak lensing shear measurement,
the Medium and Wide tier survey areas could be reduced to ensure adequate sampling.
• Shear measurements in the H-band / W-band. As discussed in Section 4.5, the multiplicative shear
bias in the W band is expected to be too large for robust weak lensing measurements due to chromatic
PSF effects; this concern is a major driver of the motivation to conduct the Wide tier observations in Hband instead of W-band. At the same time, some cosmology goals of the Wide tier have less stringent
requirements on the multiplicative shear bias than the SRD. Since shear estimation methods constantly
evolve, the initial Deep tier data should be used to re-evaluate whether the Wide tier could be conducted
in the W-band instead of the H-band, in order to yield a substantially higher number density of weaklensing source galaxies. Potentially relevant will be the lessons learned from the Euclid mission, which
relies on its wide VIS band data to measure shapes. Euclid applies mitigation techniques informed by
data periodically obtained at a set of calibration fields and strongly relies on additional photometric
information from ground.
– If the multiplicative bias for W-band can be calibrated to a sub-dominant level for cosmology inference, and W + LSST provides sufficient photo-z information (in combination with the Medium
and Deep tiers for calibration), could consider using W-band for the Wide tier. An implication of
that scenario is that the uniformity of Medium and Wide tier in the recommended survey strategy
would be altered. Other science cases that rely on more precise near-IR color information might
be impacted.
• Photometric redshift estimation and ensemble redshift distribution calibration. The recommended
design uses JHY for the Medium tier and H for the Wide tier. Grism spectroscopy requires at least two
imaging bands, and therefore covers only the Medium tier. Although Y is shallower than JH, the
current understanding of photo-z’s indicates significantly improved performance when Y is included.
The Wide tier strategy assumes that H+LSST photo-z’s can be sufficiently calibrated from the Deep
and Medium tier imaging (e.g., through Self-Organizing Maps). As more datasets come online (e.g.,
spectroscopic calibration samples, LSST and Euclid photometry), the photo-z performance should be
evaluated on the Deep fields.
– If H + LSST alone is found to be insufficient for photo-z estimation at the level needed to support
cosmology goals with the Wide tier, consider the JH imaging combination for the Wide tier over
a reduced area. If JHY + LSST is found to be required for robust photo-z estimation, consider
omitting the Wide tier and expand the area of the Medium tier, including both JHY imaging and
grism spectroscopy.
31

• Exposure time for grism spectroscopy. Use the Deep tier observations to verify that the recommended
spectroscopic strategy returns the required redshift success rate.
– If the grism spectroscopy exposure time needs to be increased to achieve the required redshift
success rate, could reduce the Wide tier area to ensure adequate exposure time while maintaining
the recommended area coverage of the Medium tier.
– If the grism spectroscopy exposure time could be reduced while maintaining the required redshift
success rate, consider increasing the area of the Medium tier; this option should be considered
together with the evaluation of photo-z estimation (see above).
• Wide Tier Survey Footprint
– Wide tier footprint could be re-optimized to account for refined understanding of backgrounds,
overlap with other datasets.
In brief, we recommend that on-orbit performance evaluation inform the final Medium and Wide tier
survey strategies to ensure that both tiers can be used for robust weak lensing measurement, while seeking to
cover the widest possible area.
In all the scenarios considered, we recommend that grism spectroscopy cover the full Medium tier footprint, and to maintain a similar 2:1 ratio of total imaging and spectroscopy time across the Medium + Wide
tiers.
Beyond the technical considerations above, the committee anticipates that the scientific landscape could
evolve between now and the early phase of the Roman mission, considering the ensemble of astronomical
surveys that are anticipated in the coming years. Combinations of Roman data with other datasets, and/or
major new scientific insights, might provide new opportunities to optimize the HLWAS strategy. In nearly all
scenarios considered by the committee, the Medium tier remains a central part of fulfilling the core Roman
cosmology mission goals. Accordingly, there is a high threshold to consider modifications to the Medium
tier. A Wide tier scheduled mainly in the latter half of the Roman mission would leave open the possibility
to re-optimize the design based on on-orbit characterization and emergent scientific opportunities that could
not be considered during the current Core Community Survey definition process.

8

Survey extensions

The white papers described science investigations ranging from the solar system to the distant universe. Many
of these investigations will be naturally enabled by our proposed HLWAS design, with some that benefit most
from the deep tier, others from the medium tier, and others from the full area of medium+wide. Other
investigations require additional data, e.g., other bands, specific targets, greater depth, or specific observing
cadence. Many of these investigations are naturally suited to GAS programs and/or to surveys that could be
executed in an extended mission. We note that ∼ 40 Msec are expected to be available for GAS proposals
during the 5-year prime mission, so many distinct surveys at the few Msec level are feasible.
Several white papers articulated the value of imaging in K213, the reddest of the Roman bands. The
recommended deep tier will include 19.2 deg2 of K213 imaging, to a 5σ point-source depth of 25.9. GAS
programs might well pursue K213 imaging over larger areas. Because of thermal background, reaching a
given mAB limit requires much longer exposures in K213 than in Y, J, or H, so the optimal tradeoff between
area and depth may be quite different for K213 imaging, depending on the priorities of the science case. Thus,
a strategy for K213 imaging is best framed in the context of a GAS program with specified science objectives,
building on the lessons learned from the HLWAS deep tier. We can also imagine GAS programs that would
add R062 imaging to a portion of the medium tier to get optical coverage at Roman spatial resolution, or a
GAS program that would obtain W146 imaging in areas not covered by the HLWAS.
Nearby galaxies are important targets because Roman imaging can resolve individual bright stars within
them. For example, imaging at the H-band depth of the HLWAS medium and wide tiers can detect TRGB
32

stars (HAB ≈ −4.5) to a distance of ∼ 10 Mpc and red clump stars (HAB ≈ 0) to slightly beyond 1 Mpc,
in the absence of crowding. The HLWAS area will encompass some nearby galaxies, including the Sculptor
group at D ∼ 4 Mpc, but a targeted survey of all galaxies with D < 10 − 15 Mpc would be a natural GAS
program. At somewhat larger distances, surface brightness fluctuations remain a useful distance indicator
and probe of stellar populations, even when individual stars can no longer be resolved. The Virgo and Coma
clusters are important targets for understanding the influence of the cluster environment on galaxy properties.
While the HLWAS will allow a useful start on nearby galaxy science, the optimal combinations of depth and
bands for such surveys will likely differ from the HLWAS choices, and they will depend on galaxy distance.
The HLWAS will span a wide range of Galactic latitude and longitude, and in combination with the
Galactic bulge and Galactic plane surveys it will enable studies of Milky Way stellar populations from the
heart of the bulge to the outer reaches of the stellar halo. Nonetheless, within the Galaxy, location matters. It
may prove valuable to complement the community surveys with GAS programs that cover different regions
of the sky or obtain second epochs of HLWAS areas for proper motion measurements. Location also matters
for solar system studies, and here the details of observing cadence are crucial to the discovery potential. The
HLWAS deep tier observations, especially in the W146 band, will provide a new census of small bodies in the
distant solar system, and it will be valuable for designing GAS surveys focused on solar system applications.
Another extension of the HLWAS could come in future spectroscopic campaigns. As discussed in Section
4.2, the cosmological FoMs for BAOs and RS Ds would be improved with additional area; if analysis of the
HLWAS data shows that spectral decontamination can be reliably achieved with imaging in a single Roman
filter, then parts of the wide-tier H-band only area could be followed up with further grism spectroscopy.
If multiple Roman bands are required, this additional area could be associated with imaging in a second
filter. Coverage of a wider area with additional spectroscopy and (potentially) multi-band imaging would
have uses for a range of other wide-field science. There is also the potential in the future to make the HLWAS
spectroscopy in the Medium and Deep fields deeper, with follow-up grism observations that could be coadded to the HLWAS spectroscopic data. The cosmological case for expanding the spectroscopic survey
would be especially strong if future observations provide compelling evidence for evolving dark energy, as
Roman is uniquely powerful at densely mapping the z > 1 universe with emission line galaxies.
As already emphasized in Section 4.4, we hope that the wide tier of the HLWAS will be the starting
point for much larger area H-band surveys in an extended mission that provide Roman-resolution imaging
over much of the sky. The science case for additional areas may prove strong enough that some of them are
proposed for GAS programs within the prime mission. In a unit that is useful for considering such programs,
we note that the survey speed of the wide tier is about 390 deg2 per Msec. At the 107 sec exposure time of
the wide tier, the overhead penalty of the HLWAS observing strategy is about 15%. This rises to a factor of
1.8 for texp = 33 sec (see Figure 1), but for some GAS science cases that require less depth than the HLWAS,
that overhead penalty may be worth paying in order to gain survey area. [For example, at texp = 33 sec,
the survey speed would be roughly 390 × (107/33) × (1.15/1.8) = 808 deg2 per Msec.] For cases that
do not require the high accuracy shape measurements of weak lensing, faster sampling strategies, e.g., one
pass with four dither positions instead of two passes with three dither positions, may also allow a better area
vs. depth optimization. For an extended mission, one might also want to reconsider the decision to do wide
tier observations in H-band rather than W146, either because there are convincing demonstrations that shape
measurement and photo-z systematics are not limiting factors for weak lensing analysis with LSST+W146,
or because weak lensing is a lower science priority by the time of the extended mission.

A

White Paper Evaluation Questions

For reference, we include the list of questions the committee considered when discussing the White Papers
and their requests for survey design:
• What are the key science goals?
• How impactful are these science goals?
33

• How central are the unique capabilities of Roman to achieving these goals?
• Does this program require coordination with other observatories or surveys, and if so how?
• How does this science program benefit from depth vs. area vs. number of bands?
• If specifying changes to the DRM:
– Relative to the DRM, what changes would be needed to accomplish the goals of this science
program?
– What bands (counting grism as a “band”) does this science program need? What depth does it
need?
• To the extent that it requires an augmentation relative to the DRM (in additional bands or additional
epochs) what area/depth is needed?
– Could this augmentation be achieved in a GO program (a reasonable limit is simtwo weeks or
∼1 Msec?)

– Does this augmentation necessarily (within reasonable observing time limits) require eliminating
some observations from the DRM?

B

HLWASDC requests to PITs and PIT reports

The HLWASDC made formal requests to both the HLIS PIT and HLSS PIT in September 2024, with reports
presented to the committee via presentations in October 2024.

B.1

HLIS PIT request

Please prepare a concise report (in the form of slides and a 30-minute presentation to the HLWAS CCS
Committee) that includes/addresses the following:
1. For dither/pass strategies that enable recovery of fully sampled shape information, over a wide range
of possible exposure times, in the Y, J, H, F184, F213, and F146 filters:
(a) Depth for point sources and rexp=0.3 arcsec extended sources
(b) Area covered per 100 days
(c) Number of effective shape measurements per 100 days
(d) Estimated median redshift
(e) Estimated number of galaxies detected at ¿ 5σ
2. For single-band, 2-band, 3-band, and 4-band strategies, with a range of exposure times and dither/pass
strategies, the WL FoM (as defined in the requirements document) attained per 100 days of observation.
If there are reasons that the FoM will not scale linearly with the total observing time, explain these
reasons.
3. For the single-band case (assumed for the wide tier), estimate the improvement in the WL FoM from
including CMB lensing data.
4. An explanation of the statistical and systematic implications of doing wide-tier imaging in F146.
5. A narrative assessment of the impact of different band choices (e.g., YJHF184, YJH only, JH only, H
only, F146 only) on photo-z accuracy and associated systematics, assuming optical imaging available
from LSST and/or Subaru HSC.
34

6. Comment on the relative advantages for HLSS cosmology of high Ecliptic latitude fields (which have
the darker sky, but would be accessible from only the Southern hemisphere if they overlap with LSST),
versus low Ecliptic latitude fields that would be accessible from both hemispheres.
7. A narrative summary of what is required from the deep-tier observations to enable adequate calibration
and systematics tests for WL cosmology, including depth, area, and bands.
8. Comments on what external calibration data (e.g., spectroscopic redshifts) will be needed to achieve
the WL cosmology goals.
9. Assuming (just as a representative, fixed number) 300 days of imaging observations allocated to
medium and wide tiers (i.e., not including deep tier observations), what is the PIT’s recommended
strategy from the perspective of WL cosmology? In particular, what is the recommended division of
time between medium and wide tiers, and what is the recommended exposure time and dither/pass
strategy within these tiers? Describe how systematics control and statistical power are factored into
this recommendation.
10. A brief narrative of how anticipated WL cosmology constraints from Roman will fit in the context of
anticipated constraints from LSST and Euclid, including issues of statistical power, systematic uncertainties, timing, and complementarity.

B.2

HLSS PIT request

Please prepare a concise report (in the form of slides and a 30-minute presentation to the HLWAS CCS
Committee) that includes/addresses the following:
1. For pass/roll-angle strategies that enable acceptably uncontaminated redshift measurements, over a
wide range of possible exposure times
(a) Line flux limits for a realistic extended source at 1.1, 1.2, 1.5, 1.8, 1.9 mu.
(b) Area covered per 100 days of spectroscopic observing.
(c) Number of z=1-2 redshifts and number of z=2-3 redshifts per 100 days.
(d) Comoving space density of galaxies with secure redshifts at z = 1.2, 1.5, 1.8, 2.2, 2.5.
(e) How does the contamination rate vary as a function of exposure time/passes/roll-angles/imaging
bands? What is the maximum contamination rate that could be effectively corrected?
2. For a range of exposure times, the BAO FoM and RSD FoM (as defined in the requirements document)
attained per 100 days of spectroscopic observation.
If there are reasons that the FoM will not scale linearly with the total observing time, explain these
reasons.
3. What is the minimum Roman imaging data needed to support the spectroscopic survey, e.g., which
bands and to what depth? Are multiple roll angles required for the imaging data, or would a single pass
with dithering that allows fully sampled images be sufficient? What is the desirable Roman imaging
data to support the spectroscopic survey, and how does it improve the redshift survey performance
relative to the minimum data? (Recognizing that more bands or greater depth will likely come at the
cost of area.)
4. Comment on the relative advantages for HLSS cosmology of high Ecliptic latitude fields (which have
the darker sky, but would be accessible from only the Southern hemisphere if they overlap with LSST),
versus low Ecliptic latitude fields that would be accessible from both hemispheres.

35

5. A narrative summary of what is required from the deep-tier observations to enable adequate calibration
and systematics tests for the galaxy redshift survey.
6. Comments on what external (non-Roman) calibration data will be needed to achieve the redshift survey’s cosmology goals.
7. Assuming (just as a representative, fixed number) 150 days of wide-area spectroscopic observations
(i.e., not including the deep tier), what is the PIT’s recommended survey strategy from the perspective
of redshift survey cosmology? Should the spectroscopic survey adopt a uniform exposure time or have
medium and wide tiers with different exposure times and/or observing strategies? What should the
exposure time or times be? Describe how systematics control and statistical power are factored into
this recommendation.
8. A brief narrative of how anticipated redshift survey cosmology constraints from Roman will fit in
the context of anticipated constraints from DESI and Euclid, including issues of statistical power,
systematic uncertainties, timing, and complementarity.

High Latitude Time Domain Survey

Findings of the Roman High-Latitude Time-Domain
Survey Definition Committee
S. Bradley Cenko1 , Masao Sako2 (Co-Chairs), Susan Benecchi3 ,
Alessandra Corsi4 , Michael Fausnaugh5 , Sebastian Gomez6 ,
Rebekah Hounsell7 , Takashi Moriya8 , Rosemary Pike9 , Gordon
Richards10 , Russell Ryan11 , Schuyler Van Dyk12 , and V. Ashley
Villar13
13 December 2024
The High Latitude Time Domain Survey (HLTDS) is one of three Core
Community Surveys that Roman will carry out during its nominal
5-year mission. The HLTDS Survey Definition Committee was charged
with designing a survey strategy that meets the scientific requirements
related to Type Ia supernova (SN Ia) cosmology while also maximizing
the broader scientific return for the entire Roman community.
Here we describe our recommended implementation of the HLTDS,
which has three components:
• The Core Component (HLTDS-CC): Modeled in many ways after
the Design Reference Survey (Rose et al. 2021), the HLTDS-CC is the
primary source of discovery and characterization for Type Ia SNe
and most other transient/variable source classes. The HLTDS-CC
requires the bulk of the survey time to perform cadenced imaging
and prism spectroscopy over the central two years of the five-year
prime Roman operations.
• The Pilot Component (HLTDS-PC): As the HLTDS-CC does not begin until 1.5 years into Roman operations, our committee felt that
obtaining imaging and prism spectroscopy of our recommended
fields is critical early in the mission lifetime. The HLTDS-PC provides deep, supernova-free reference observations of the HLTDS-CC
fields, as well as an opportunity to test assumptions that drove our
survey design recommendations (e.g., high-z SN Ia rate) and data
analysis pipelines.
• The Extended Component (HLTDS-EC): In addition to SN Ia cosmology, the NIR sensitivity will make Roman a particularly powerful probe of transients in the early Universe. Due both to cosmological time dilation and intrinsic physics, many of these high-z sources
will have long time scales that are difficult to fully capture with the
two-year duration of the HLTDS-CC. We therefore also recommend
a (temporally) extended component to provide access to the longest
time scales possible in the prime Roman mission lifetime.

Science Motivation
Science Requirements and Figure of Merit
The primary goal of the HLTDS is to use SNe Ia as cosmological
probes to measure the expansion history of the Universe. The light
curves of SNe Ia are powerful “standardizable” candles: measurement of their peak luminosity, evolution time scale, and color can
be used to infer reliable distances to these sources14 . As a result,
SNe Ia offered the first evidence of the accelerating expansion of the
Universe, commonly attributed to dark energy15 .
With its large field-of-view, exceptional sensitivity, and NIR bandpass, Roman will be by far the most efficient discovery machine of
SNe Ia at z > 1. Formally, the requirements for Roman SN Ia cosmology are stated in terms of the Dark Energy Task Force Figure of Merit
(FoM)16 :
FoM ≡ det[C(w0 , wa )],
(1)
where C is the covariance matrix of the equation of state parameters
w0 and wa (Figure 1). When combined with other complementary
cosmological probes (e.g., weak lensing, baryon acoustic oscillations),
the HLTDS requirement is to achieve FoM > 325.

possibility to address a broad range of topics at the forefront of
modern (astro)physics. The recent explosion in monitoring of the

time-variable sky – particularly at optical wavelengths – has revealed
entirely (and in some cases unexpected) phenomena, and features
prominently as a community priority in the 2020 Decadal Survey.
NIR observations by Roman are particularly well suited to identify
high-redshift transients, dust-extinguished sources, and cool/red
objects. Furthermore, the NIR transient sky has been less well explored to date17 , offering the opportunity for serendipitous discovery.
Optimizing the broad science return from the HLTDS, while simultaneously achieving the SN Ia cosmology requirements, is the “dual
mandate” at the heart of our committee work18 .

White Papers, Science Pitches, and Community Feedback
To ensure that committee considered the broadest range of possible
science in defining the HLTDS, multiple opportunities for community
input were provided.
During the call for White Papers we received a total of 23 papers
related to the HLTDS19 . These papers include recommendations for
several modifications to the Design Reference Mission implementation of the HLTDS20 , with particular focus on optimizations on filter
selection, cadence, depth, survey area, and field choices. Additionally, some recommendations for synergies with other observatories
and Roman surveys were proposed. Lastly, a number of papers advocate for an extended HLTDS that covers a period longer than the
nominal 2 years of the survey.
Most of these modifications are aimed at enchaining the detection,
classification, and characterization of a number of time-variable astrophysical sources, including: Type Ia SNe, superluminous supernovae
(SLSNe), high-redshift SNe, pair-instability SNe (PISNe), tidal disruption events (TDEs), kilonovae (KNe), active galactic nuclei (AGN),
variable stars, and black hole binaries. Some papers also advocate
for the optimization of the survey towards the study of high-redshift
galaxies, resolved stellar populations in nearby galaxies, and low
mass stars in the Milky Way halo.
A majority of papers emphasize the need for careful optimization
of filter selection. The most frequently recommended filters are F158
(H), F184 (F), and F213 (K), with various combinations suggested
to enhance specific science cases. For example, Joshi, Harikane, and
Moriya highlight these filters are necessary to improve measurements
of high-redshift galaxies, stellar mass assembly, and detecting rare
phenomena such as pair-instability supernovae (PISNe), respectively.
Additionally, Kraemer and Holwerda suggest these filters are needed
for accurate distance measurements and characterization of low-mass
stars.

19

See https://asd.gsfc.nasa.gov/
roman/wps_2023 for a full listing.
20

B. M. Rose et al. A Reference
Survey for Supernova Cosmology with the Nancy Grace Roman
Space Telescope. arXiv e-prints, art.
arXiv:2111.03081, November 2021. doi:
10.48550/arXiv.2111.03081

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

Several papers propose adjustments to the cadence of the survey,
aimed towards the detection and characterization of transients with
varying timescales. For instance, Macias and Andreoni advocate
for at least part of the survey to have fast cadences under 4 days
to accommodate rapidly evolving transients. Rose and Fox echo
the need for a staggered or decoupled cadence to better capture
SNe, KNe, and high-redshift events such as SLSNe, TDEs, or PISNe.
Lastly, Rose proposed a one-day cadence for prism spectroscopy to
maximize transient discovery.
Several papers, such as Gomez, Fox, Yamada, and Moriya, suggest
extending the survey duration beyond the nominal 2 years. These
authors argue that longer survey durations, up to 5 years, would
allow for the detection and characterization of rarer and more exotic
events, such as low-luminosity AGN, SNe at very high redshifts (z >
5), PISNe, TDEs, and SLSNe.
In terms of synergies with other observatories, Thilker, Harikane,
and Koyama propose leveraging Roman’s infrared capabilities alongside other telescopes, such as CASTOR and Subaru. These synergies
could enhance studies of galaxy evolution, structure formation, and
reionization. Similarly, Rhoads and Haiman emphasize the importance of overlapping the HLTDS with the Roman HLWAS to improve
studies of AGN variability and black hole seed evolution.
Lastly, a number of papers touch on the subject of field selection.
Rose and Aldering emphasize the importance of choosing fields
with low zodiacal background to maximize sensitivity and improve
data quality, particularly for transient and supernova cosmology
studies. The recommendation to focus on fields in the Continuous
Viewing Zone (CVZ), such as the Euclid Deep Field South or the
Extended Groth Strip, ensures that these fields are observed with
high frequency and minimal interruption. Additionally, Harikane
and Koyama advocate for selecting fields that overlap with Subaru to
enhance synergies towards the study of galaxy evolution and highredshift studies. Kraemer advocates for choosing a field that contains
some nearby galaxies to significantly improve the cosmic distance
ladder by observing long-period variable stars in these galaxies.
Deustua recommends selecting calibration fields to improve the precision of all Roman measurements, critical for supernova cosmology.
In addition to the White Papers, 23 Science Pitches were also
submitted for the HLTDS21 . Many of these were followed up with
(longer) White Papers, but a significant number were not.
Goobar stresses that strongly lensed SNe Ia will provide additional
constraints on cosmological parameters, while Shajib emphasizes that
early detection of lensed SNe Ia in the HLTDS is crucial. Yahalomi
discusses how precision astrometry afforded by Roman, together

See https://asd.gsfc.nasa.gov/
roman/sci_pitch/ for a full listing.

with ground-based radial velocity observations, could detect solar
system analogs in the Survey fields. Pierel argues that inclusion of
the F814W (Z) and F213W (K) bands, in a wide field and deep field,
respectively, are essential for obtaining data for thousands of restframe near-IR SNe Ia, which are less affected by host galaxy dust,
as well as increasing the parameter space probed by the HLTDS
for all transients. Fausnaugh points out that the HLTDS provides a
unique opportunity to perform AGN reverberation mapping. Suzuki
advocates for coverage by the HLTDS of the Chandra Deep Field
South. Nugent describes synergies between the La Silla Schmidt
Southern Survey (LS4) with LSST and a possible southern HLTDS
field. Hickox describes how the HLTDS will be particularly valuable
for monitoring low-mass AGN. Finally, Gomez makes a generalized
pitch of how invaluable the HLTDS will be for studying all types of
transient phenomena.

Committee Process
The approach adopted by our committee was driven by two guiding
principles:
1. A desire to enable the broadest range of science possible, in particular science uniquely possible with Roman, given the constraints
of needing to satisfy the type Ia SNe cosmology requirements. In
our discussions it quickly became clear that (aside from SN Ia cosmology) there was no single science case that stood apart. Rather,
an extremely wide range of pressing questions in (astro)physics
could be addressed by a well-designed HLTDS, ranging from cosmology (e.g., lensed SNe) to reionization and the early Universe
(e.g., high-redshift pair instability SNe) to the physics of accretion
(e.g., AGN variability, tidal disruption events) and stellar evolution
(e.g., core-collapse SNe). That said, there were clearly science cases
that could be uniquely addressed by the large field-of-view and
NIR sensitivity of Roman, and our committee prioritized those
topics.
2. Given the limited time available to arrive at our recommendations,
as well as the significant effort that had been put into this topic
previously22 , the committee attempted to leverage external efforts
as much as possible. This took multiple forms over the course of
our work. We made repeated efforts to engage with the broader
community, from the initial Science Pitches and White Papers, to
outreach at scientific conferences (AAS, Roman Science Conference at Caltech), to the time-domain community working group
(STRIDE). We interacted closely with the RAPID and SN Cosmol-

22

R. Hounsell et al. Simulations of
the WFIRST Supernova Survey and
Forecasts of Cosmological Constraints.
ApJ, 867(1):23, November 2018. doi:
10.3847/1538-4357/aac08b; and B. M.
Rose et al. A Reference Survey for
Supernova Cosmology with the Nancy
Grace Roman Space Telescope. arXiv eprints, art. arXiv:2111.03081, November
2021. doi: 10.48550/arXiv.2111.03081

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

ogy Project Infrastructure Teams. And we were fortunate to have
close collaboration with the Roman Project Team, including members at Goddard Space Flight Center, the Space Telescope Science
Institute, and Caltech/IPAC.
Towards these ends, our first task was to familiarize ourselves with
the type Ia SNe cosmology requirements. Committee members read
the Science Requirements Document (RST-SYS-REQ-0020, Revision
D) and received a presentation from the Roman Project team on
performance details relevant to the SN Ia cosmology science.
Second, we learned about the efforts undertaken by the two previous SN Ia Science Investigation Teams. We carefully examined the
Design Reference Mission described in Rose et al. (2021), in order to
better understand the trade offs that were considered as part of this
effort.
Next, we read the Science Pitches and White Papers that were
flagged as relevant to the HLTDS. All Science Pitches and White Papers were assigned at least 3 readers on the committee, and discussed
during committee deliberations. Where necessary individual authors
were contacted to provide additional information.
At this stage, the committee’s efforts were split into four different
sub-groups, each of which worked in parallel but reported progress
to the entire committee at biweekly Zoom meetings. The four subgroups were:
• Imaging Implementation: Responsible for defining the detailed
survey strategy (filters used, areal coverage, depth, and cadence)
for the imaging portion of the HLTDS-CC
• Spectroscopy Implementation: Responsible for defining the detailed survey strategy (fraction of time, areal coverage, and depth)
for the spectroscopic portion of the HLTDS-CC
• Field Selection: Responsible for defining the specific fields
utilized for the HLTDS-CC
• Survey Extensions: Responsible for examining potential survey
extensions, including reference imaging / a pilot survey early in
the Roman prime mission phase, extended observations over the
entire Roman prime mission phase, and possible F213W (K-band)
observations.
Finally, the findings from the different working groups were synthesized into a single, comprehensive set of recommendations for the
implementation of the HLTDS.

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

Discussion of Results
Imaging Implementation
Designing an optimal and effective imaging component for the
HLTDS involves balancing the unique capabilities of Roman and
accounting for existing data sets as well as concurrent surveys, in
particular, the Rubin Observatory’s Legacy Survey of Space and Time
(LSST).
To optimize the choice of cadence, filters, depth, area, and number
of tiers, we relied on both simulations and the committee’s collective
expert knowledge of transient science. Simulations are necessary to
qualitatively study the trade-offs between the various survey parameters. However, it is also important to account for the assumptions
embedded in the simulations. For example, simulations alone tend
to favor large areas and a limited number of filters to maximize the
number of supernovae, neglecting unknown systematics.
Rubin’s LSST is scheduled to begin survey operations in mid-tolate 2025. Their Deep Drilling Fields (DDFs) will monitor ≈ 100 deg2
over 10 years, yielding many thousands of high-quality SN Ia light
curves with median redshift z ≈ 0.6. To complement this data set, it
is desirable to target substantially higher redshifts with Roman and
to observe in the reddest bands possible.
We ran a large grid of survey simulations, varying the cadence,
areal coverage, depth, and filters used, while maintaining a fixed
overall survey time23 . With help from the SN Project Infrastructure
Team, the simulations were run using both the SNANA simulation
package24 , as well a forecasting package provided by D. Rubin, as a
cross-check for consistency.
To evaluate the efficacy of the simulations, we estimated the resulting DETF FoM under the following set of assumptions:
• The Roman SN Ia data set was combined with a nearby sample of
800 “anchor” SNe Ia at z < 0.1, as well as a high-quality intermediate redshift sample of 3,500 ugrizY SNe Ia light curves from
Rubin.
• Cosmic microwave background (CMB) prior based on the R-shift
parameter computed from the input cosmology – flat ΛCDM with
Ωm = 0.315. An uncertainty of σR = 0.0044 is assumed.
• The systematic uncertainties included are: a) 0.005 mag zeropoint
error for Roman and LSST, b) 5 Å error on mean filter wavelength
for Roman and LSST, c) 0.0071 mag Å−1 in global calibration, d)
nonlinearity of 0.05% over 4.5 dex (Roman only), e) 0.01 shift in
host photo-z’s (Roman only), f) 20% increase in σ (host-photo-z)

23
Set to 180 d, the entire in-guide
allocation for the HLTDS.
24

Richard Kessler et al. SNANA: A
Public Software Package for Supernova Analysis. PASP, 121(883):1028,
September 2009. doi: 10.1086/605984

8

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

9

(Roman only), g) 4 × 10−5 shift in spec-z (LSST only), and h) 5%
error in Galactic E( B − V ) for Roman and LSST.
We emphasize that the absolute values of the resulting DETF FoMs
depend sensitively on these assumptions, and as a result there
is strong motivation to provide significant margin in the survey
design to meet the Roman SN Ia cosmology requirements. For
example, for identical survey designs, the resulting DETF FoM varied
by as much as 5–10% just between the different simulation packages.
However, the relative FoM values comparing different survey designs
were generally found to be robust.
Under the above set of assumptions, the DETF FoM was generally
maximized in survey designs with at least two imaging tiers25 , each
with 4-5 filters, and a cadence of 8–10 d. However, within this general area of survey space, the variation in DETF FoM was relatively
modest (i.e., a shallow maximum), and a broad range of strategies
appeared viable. As a result, considerations other than the DETF
FoM (e.g., non-SN Ia science) could have an appreciable impact on
the HLTDS design.
We recommend two distinct imaging tiers – Wide and Deep – that
target SN Ia at z = 0.9 and z = 1.7 respectively. The Wide Tier is
imaged in the RZYJH filters, while the Deep Tier utilizes ZYJHF. The
combination of two tiers and the selected filters enables sufficient
coverage in both wavelength and redshift space, allowing for the collection of a broad and statistically robust sample of SN Ia (Figure 2).
We verified this filter complement also enables robust photometric
classification of transient sources, which will be critical given the
depths reached by the HLTDS in the NIR.

25

To target intermediate- and highredshift SNe Ia separately.

We considered adding K-band (F213W) observations to the HLTDSCC; however, the dramatically reduced sensitivity26 made it infeasible for routine observations. We reconsider a (significantly) reduced
cadence of K-band observations as part of the possible Survey Extensions below.
We also note that while there is significant overlap in the redshift
distribution between the Wide and Deep Tiers (Figure 2), targeting
a lower redshift for the Wide Tier would result in additional overlap
with the Rubin sample (in terms of both redshift and filter coverage), thus not taking full advantage of the NIR sensitivity offered
by Roman. The larger areal coverage would also reduce the survey
efficiency due to increased slew times. Furthermore, light curves in
the Deep Imaging Tier have significantly higher S/N compared to the
Wide Imaging Tier at a given redshift.
Because of the relatively long time scale associated with SNe Ia27 ,
the DETF FoM was generally maximized for imaging cadences ≥10 d
(Figure 3). While longer cadences may be preferred by the simulations, these neglect systematic uncertainties in the light curve model,
and thus a somewhat higher cadence is preferable for cosmological
analyses. Furthermore, even cadences of 10 d would miss some of
the exciting fast-evolving non-SN Ia transients that Roman can, in
principle, uniquely probe (e.g., kilonova28 ).

For these reasons, rather than observing the complete filter set
every 10 d, we recommend splitting the filters into two sets and observing each with a cadence of 5 d. The bluest filter of each tier is
observed each visit - i.e., in the Wide, we observe in RZJ on day 0
and RYH on day 5; in the deep, ZYH on day 0 and ZJF on day 5. This
interlaced strategy allows us to capture rapidly evolving transients

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

in addition to SN Ia. The repetition of the bluest filter in each tier
will also provide a method of differentiating color vs. flux evolution,
classification, and potentially improve photo-z calculations. The visits
do not need to be exactly 5 days apart29 . We can tolerate ∆ ≈ 1 day
as long as the mean is 5 d.
The exposure time in a given filter is set to achieve a fixed S/N
near maximum light and it is determined by the cadence and target
redshift according to the following formula:
s
cadence
S/N = 10
.
(2)
2.5(1 + ztarget )
So in a ±5-day-range around maximum light, there will be an average S/N of 20 for the median SN at that redshift. The exposure times
per visit are shown in Table 1.
Dithering was given serious consideration by the committee. Splitting the exposures in each visit to 2-3 will help with cosmic-ray rejection, smoothing out detector defects, and filling in gaps between detectors. However, dithering also adds overhead (slew/settle time plus
added exposure time to offset read noise), which is significant given
the relatively short exposure times in most of our filters. We therefore
choose not to dither except possibly in the Deep Tier F-band. Slight
dithers across visits are favorable (i.e., not rotating around a fixed
axis) to avoid circular, onion-like patterns in the exposure map.
In addition to the SN Ia cosmology results described above, the
HLTDS-CC will be a rich source of non-SN Ia transients and variables. Figures 4 and 5 highlight some of the additional source classes
that can be recovered using the HLTDS-CC survey strategy described
here.

Spectroscopy Implementation
A unique capability of Roman is cadenced spectroscopy of transients
discovered in the HLTDS. Spectroscopic monitoring of transients
with Roman’s prism can potentially be used to improve SN Ia distances, classify transients, build templates and training sets for a
wide variety of transients, and provide spectroscopic monitoring of
extragalactic variables from SN to AGN.
The main trade for slitless spectroscopy with Roman’s prism is
the survey area, because the prism requires relatively long exposure
times. The DETF FoM scales strongly with the survey area, due to
the increasing number of SN Ia discovered in larger solid angles. On
the surface, there appears to be a tension with the primary goal of
the HLTDS and the spectroscopic observations. However, recent work
suggests that cadenced spectroscopy can improve SN Ia standardization and decrease the impact of systematic errors30 . The magnitude
of these gains is disputed in the literature31 , and so the balance of
spectroscopy is a complicated decision. While the known uncertainties in SN Ia cosmology favor minimizing the prism time in order
to maximize the SN Ia sample, poorly constrained or hitherto undetected systematic effects might benefit from increased prism time.
The working group considered 3 main lines of questioning: (1)
What are the main benefits to the cosmology analysis from SN Ia
spectra? (2) What science cases, if any, required faster cadence with
the prism than provided in the reference survey? (3) What other
science cases would most benefit from increasing the fraction of
prism time?
(1) What are the main benefits to the cosmology analysis

13

from SN Ia spectra?

Spectroscopic time series of SN Ia have the potential for improved
standardization of SN Ia light curves. Recent work suggests that
there is a 3-parameter space embedded in SN Ia spectra near-peak
that predicts the SN Ia peak brightness32 . This method of standardization may also reduce systematic errors, such as correlations of host
galaxy properties with peak magnitude. A variety of methods have
been used to investigate the 3D parameter space, but so far (a) the
main physical parameters have not been identified, and (b) the magnitude of the improvement for cosmological analysis has not been
settled.
The prism is not sensitive enough to provide a large training set
(with high SNR) that is comparable to state-of-the-art spectroscopic
analysis from ground-based surveys. However, the spectroscopic
standardization models can be applied to the Roman SN Ia with
prism observations, there will be several thousand SN Ia with time
series spectroscopic observations, and we expect a sample up to 20
times larger than that of existing SN Ia samples used in spectroscopic
standardization analyses (Figure 6). Roman therefore presents an
exciting possibility for investigating and characterizing systematic
uncertainties in SN Ia distances using spectroscopy.
A forecasting analysis suggests that the FoM is roughly constant
with prism fraction when using spectroscopic standardization. The
size of the SN Ia sample with prism observations increases roughly
linearly with prism fraction, and these SN make significant improvements to the FoM. However, the total survey area decreases, which
decreases the photometric sample and puts downwards pressure on
the FoM. Meanwhile, the photometric analysis generally gives a decreasing FoM for increasing prism time, because the total area of the
survey is smaller.
To choose the total time spent on prism, we picked a value that
would yield a good FoM using either photometric standardization
(SALT333 ) or spectroscopic standardization. Based on a suite of simulations, we found that about 14% of the total Core Component time
used for prism observations yields a FoM in the top 7% of simulations using photometric analysis, and that 0% prism time gives only
a 2% improvement in the FoM. On the other hand, spectroscopic
analysis for a 28% prism CC fraction improves the FoM by 2% compared to a 14% prism fraction. These ranges (14 to 28 per cent) bound
the problem, because prism fractions below 14% or above 28% have
a relatively small impact on the FoM for the same analysis, but a
prism fraction below 14% would limit the sample of SNIa with spectroscopy, while a prism fraction above 28% would limit the total SN
Ia sample size. We therefore propose ≈20% of the total CC survey

of Spectra at Maximum Light. ApJ, 912
(1):70, May 2021b. doi: 10.3847/15384357/abec3c

time to be used for prism observations.
(2) What science cases, if any, require faster cadence spectroscopy
than provided in the Design Reference Mission?

The working group did not identify any science cases that would
strongly benefit from a spectroscopic cadence faster than 5 days. The
bulk of transients discovered in the survey are at redshifts z > 0.5,
and an increase in prism time for faster cadence could not be justified
for the very small number of sources that would be discovered at low
redshift. At the intermediate and high redshifts, science requiring
fast cadence can be accomplished with the a 5 day (observer frame)
cadence (i.e., rapidly fading transients, early time observations of SN
close to the time of explosion, binary supermassive black holes in
AGN at short periods). Other high luminosity variables tend to have
longer timescales of variability (i.e., weeks to months for AGN), and
so a cadence faster than 5 days is not needed.
(3) What other science cases would most benefit from large
fractions of prism time?

The other major science cases for the Roman prism are classification/characterization of transients and static science. The key metric
for classification is the total yield of objects with reliable classifications. One White Paper (Aldering et al.) noted that Roman has the
unique capability to spectroscopically follow-up its own transients,
which is a challenge from the ground owing to the faint magnitudes
and other factors, such as hemisphere observability, operational status of a given observatory, and weather. Cadenced spectroscopy is
also a unique opportunity to look at the detailed spectral evolution of
transients.

15

One difficulty, however, is the sensitivity of the prism, which limits
the SNR of the majority of objects in the field. This limitation is offset
to some extent by the slow evolution of the transients at high redshift. By combining spectra across the time series for each object, the
effective SNR of each object will be higher for purposes of classification and characterization. Robust analysis tools using the time-series
spectra are not yet standard in the field, but acquiring the time-series
spectra in the HLTDS-CC will make it possible to classify a larger
number of objects if such tools become more widespread.
The total yield of transients favors a larger area of prism observations, while better SNR for individual objects favors a smaller field
with longer exposure times. Within the 20% limit on prism fraction
imposed by the SN Ia and cosmology science, we explored the relative yields and SNRs of both strategies. We found that it is better
to include both a wide and a deep prism tier. The wide tier yields
a large sample of transients with spectroscopy, while the deep tier
yields a reasonable number of transients with higher SNR spectra as
well as a sample pushing to redshifts greater than 1.0.

To set the parameters of the prism wide and deep tiers, we turn
to additional survey constraints. The area covered by prism observations will be small because the prism exposures must be long. Roman
pointings should be set in an axisymmetric pattern, so that rotations of the telescope over the year minimize edge effects. For small
fields, this results in a “quantization” effect, where certain preset patterns clearly optimize the overall area: examples are a 1 × 2 pointing
scheme for 0.56 deg2 (6×6 detectors) or a 2 × 3 + 1 pointing scheme
for 1.68 deg2 (12×9 detectors), see Figure 7.
For the deep prism tier (target redshift 1.7, exposure time = 3600 s),
covering 0.56 deg2 requires 7% of the CC survey time, while 1.68 deg2
requires 20% of the total CC survey time. Given a maximum of 20%
of the prism time and the need for both a wide and deep tier, the

deep prism tier should be 0.56 deg2 , using 7% of the total CC survey

Both tiers will be embedded in the larger HLTDS-CC Deep Imaging
Tier (see below).

Field Selection
Two White Papers – Rose and Aldering – summarize the viable field
choices for the HLTDS-CC (Figure 8). The fields are required to be
in the Roman Continuous Viewing Zone (CVZ), corresponding to
ecliptic latitude |ϵ| > 54◦ . Furthermore, to maximize the transient
discovery rate, minimal foreground extinction is preferred. Complementary pre-existing and contemporaneous multi-wavelength data
sets are strongly desired. And an additional challenge is the relative
merit of a Northern vs. Southern field choice.

Figure 8: Possible field selections for
the HLTDS-CC (equatorial projection).
The fields must reside in the CVZ
(|ϵ| > 54◦ ), should have minimal
foreground extinction, and maximal
pre-existing and contemporaneous
multi-wavelength coverage. Credit:
David Rubin

We concluded that splitting time between the North and South
would be prudent. By widely separating the fields in right ascension,
this ensures that at least some area of the HLTDS-CC is visible to
ground-based observers year-round. Two distinct areas provide the
possibility of making use of the Subaru’s contribution to Roman and
overlap with Rubin34 , and also enable the possibility of a jack-knife
test for the SN Ia cosmological analysis, i.e. to mitigate against the
possibility of cosmic variance. Of course, however, additional slew
time would be incurred.
In the North, the ELAIS-N1 field35 was felt to be the logical choice
(Figure 9). ELIAS-N1 has been observed in various wavelength
ranges 36 . Deep optical data have been obtained by the Subaru/HSC
SSP Deep Survey (grizy) and PanSTARRS Medium Deep Survey
(grizy). The Subaru/HSC SSP Deep Survey also provides some narrow filter observations (N387, N816, N921). Deep infrared data in

ELAIS-N1 are obtained by UKIDSS (JHK) and the Spitzer SWIRE and
SERVS surveys (3.6um, 4.5um, 5.6um, 8um, 24um, 70um, 160um).
The u band data are available from the SpARCS survey of CFHT.
GALEX obtained deep images in the NUV and FUV bands in this
field. Deep X-ray data are available from Chandra and deep radio
data are available from LOFAR.
Furthermore, ELAIS-N1 is well-positioned for observations with
Subaru, and the foreground extinction is very low (AV = 0.02 mag).

In the South, the most logical choices are the LSST Deep Drilling
Fields (DDFs), with CDFS and COSMOS being far and away the best
choices from the perspective of synergy with LSST synoptic data;
CDFS for its wealth of multi-wavelength data, and COSMOS for the
combination of multi-wavelength data and plans for accelerated (and
extended) time-domain coverage (10-years of coverage in 3-years,
plus continued monitoring for the remaining 7 years). However,
neither are in the Roman CVZ. COSMOS is quite far away from the
CVZ; CDFS is much closer and might be re-considered if the CVZ
constraint could be slightly loosened.
With that said, the next most logical choice is the Euclid Deep
Field South (EDFS). LSST will observe it with 2 pointings (so, double the area of the other LSST DDFs), but at only half the cumulative
depth, in order to keep the total time the same as the other DDFs. So
the time domain synergy with Roman is weaker than for CDFS. Nevertheless, the combination of Roman and Rubin in the EDFS would

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

18

provide a unique combination of cadence and wavelength coverage
that would enable much science beyond the core SN program.
In terms of wide vs. deep, it was noted that the overlap region of
the EDFS would have double the time coverage and greater depth
than the rest of the LSST EDFS, thus the overlap region would make
for a logical location for the deep portion of the HLTDS-CC. One important caveat is the lack of deep X-ray data to complement the deep
LSST+Roman photometry (as compared to what one might have for
CDFS). We hope that the selection of this field for the HLTDS-CC
will motivate additional multi-wavelength coverage of the EDFS (Figure 10).

Survey Extensions
The committee considered a variety of extensions to the HLTDS-CC –
longer (and shorter) temporal baselines, wavelength augmentations,
etc. Here we summarize these discussions.

Figure 10: This is a corn field in Iowa.
People who get this reference about
motivating EDFS X-ray coverage probably also like root beer and peanut
butter.

Pilot Component: The primary HLTDS-CC will collect data only in

the central 2 years of the 5-year prime Roman lifetime. While it is
necessary for the HLTDS-CC to be finite in duration37 , waiting a full
18 months to collect any HLTDS data carries significant risk for the
successful achievement of the Roman SN cosmology goals. Our
committee therefore felt strongly that a Pilot Component (HLTDSPC) should be included in the in-guide survey.
Motivations for the HLTDS-PC are myriad, including:

37

To accommodate the several monthlong continuous observations of microlensing Bulge fields.

• Deep reference images are required to remove underlying host
galaxy emission in SN light curves, and these are most effective
when they lack any SN light.
• The rate of SNe Ia at z > 1 is highly uncertain with individual
measurements ranging by a factor of ∼ 2 with similar uncertainties38 , and the DETF FoM depends sensitively on acquiring a
sufficiently large high-z sample. Measuring this rate before the
HLTDS-CC would allow adjustments (e.g., more or less area in the
Deep Tier) should they be necessary.
• To increase efficiency, our proposed in-guide HLTDS-CC lacks any
spatial dithering. An HLTDS-PC would verify on-sky if bona fide
transients can be distinguished from cosmic rays in single epoch
(but multi-filter) visits.
• Prism data obtained at multiple roll angles will enable testing of
host-galaxy subtraction and/or forward-modeling software to
extract the SN spectrum. These data will also serve as templates
for SN discovered in the Core Component.

38

Louis-Gregory Strolger et al. Delay
Time Distributions of Type Ia Supernovae from Galaxy and Cosmic Star
Formation Histories. ApJ, 890(2):140,
February 2020. doi: 10.3847/15384357/ab6a97

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

• An HLTDS-PC could be used to train machine-learning transient
classifiers for Roman. While a number of such classifiers have
been developed for Rubin39 , the lack of sensitive, wide-field NIR
surveys has largely precluded this development for Roman.
• The HLTDS-PC observations can be used to test data reduction
pipelines and analysis tools, which is particularly important for
the real-time notifications planned by the RAPID Project Infrastructure Team.

39

V. Ashley Villar et al. SuperRAENN:
A Semisupervised Supernova Photometric Classification Pipeline Trained
on Pan-STARRS1 Medium-Deep Survey
Supernovae. ApJ, 905(2):94, December
2020. doi: 10.3847/1538-4357/abc6fd

• HLTDS-PC will also enable early non-transient deep-field science and follow-up spectroscopy such as discovery of the highestredshifts galaxies and AGN, studying the epoch of reionization,
measurement of the UV luminosity function and other static science discussed in several White Papers.
At least ≈8 individual epochs at the depth of a single HLTDS-PC
visit are necessary to construct a sufficiently deep reference image
that its noise contribution will be negligible. We simulated different
cadences for the HLTDS-PC, ranging from 10–30 d. While the 10 d
cadence would match that from the HLTDS-CC, our simulations
indicated that a 3-month duration40 for the HLTDS-PC would be
very inefficient, in that many SNe Ia at the beginning and end would
not have sufficient light curve coverage for cosmological analysis
(particularly those at high-z). At the other end, a 30 d cadence was
found to be too infrequent for reliable classification, and would be
extremely difficult to schedule with the Bulge Survey fields. Instead
we recommend 8 visits at a 20 d cadence for the HLTDS-PC for both
imaging and prism spectroscopy. All other aspects of the HLTDS-PC
would mimic those of the HLTDS-CC (area, filters, exposure times,
etc.).
Extended Component: Roman will be uniquely capable of detect-

ing some of the dimmest and therefore highest-redshift transients
ever discovered. Moreover, Roman will be able to systematically
explore their population by discovering multiples of these in a systematic way. One limitation of the HLTDS-CC is its relatively short
nominal duration of two years. For the longest-duration transients,
the HLTDS-CC would likely miss their rise or decline, preventing
characterization. Here we explore the possibility of an Extended
Component (HLTDS-EC), which would increase the duration of the
HLTDS by at least 2.5 years at a much slower cadence of at most
90 days. This survey is optimized to discover the most luminous,
longest duration, and highest redshift transients. The suggestion for
an HLTDS-EC was motivated by a number of White Papers (Fox,
Gomez, Karmen, Moriya, Yamada).
The HLTDS-EC is designed to answer one of the biggest questions

19

40

10 d × 10 epochs.

findings of the roman high-latitude time-domain survey definition committee

20

in time domain astronomy: how do transients evolve with redshift?
Discovering and characterizing transients as a function of redshift
will motivate studies of both stellar evolution and black hole evolution. By measuring the physical parameters and rates of SNe as a
function of redshift we can make inferences about the IMF. Similarly,
measuring the masses of supermassive black holes (SMBHs) can tell
us about their formation and how they impact their galaxies. We focus our exploration of the HLTDS-EC by determining how well these
observations can characterize tidal disruption events (TDEs), superluminous supernovae (SLSNe), and pair-instability supernovae (PISNe).
These transients are chosen given that they are the most luminous
and longest-duration explosive transients known, and directly relevant to the scientific questions of stellar and black hole evolution
(Figure 11; Gomez, Moriya, Karmen).

We define a relative EC FoM for each type of transient (SLSNe,
PISNe, TDEs) as the ratio of the number of these objects that can
be discovered by adding the HLTDS-EC compared to the HLTDSCC alone. We consider a transient to have enough photometry to
be adequately characterized if it is detected in at least two epochs
before peak, one during peak, and at least three epochs after peak.
Additionally, we require the detections to span at least 30 rest-frame
days in coverage. We derive the corresponding limits on the survey
using Pandeia 2024.9.1.
We find that the reddest filters are the most optimal for detecting
these high-redshift, long-duration transients. The most effective filter
for detecting these is F158W (H), followed by F184W (F). Four filters
provide enough color information to characterize the transient. The
addition of F213W (K) is greatly beneficial to differentiate types of
transients (Moriya Whitepaper) – however, it is also significantly
less sensitive41 We therefore consider F213W (K) band observations
separately below.
Our simulations indicate that: 1) A temporal extension to the

21

HLTDS-CC can have a dramatic impact (EC FoM ∼2–3) on the
discovery of high-z transients such as TDEs, SLSNe, and PISNe
(Figure 12); 2) The EC FoM benefits more from a longer temporal
baseline than increased cadence. Therefore we recommend that the
HLTDS-EC be included in the in-guide request, and that it cover as
long a period of time as possible – at least 2, and up to 3 years, before
and after the HLTDS-CC. A cadence of 120 d is sufficient to more
than double the number of discoveries of these critical sources.
However, given the focus on the high-z universe, and the lack of
the reddest filters employed in the HLTDS-CC Wide Imaging Tier, we
recommend the HLTDS-EC only cover the Deep Imaging Tier fields
with an identical observing strategy as that employed for the HLTDSCC. After accounting for recommended in-guide observations from
both the HLTDS-CC and HLTDS-PC, the HLTDS-EC would require
∼ 8 additional epochs.

We also note that the addition of visits in the HLTDS-EC will also
recover some SN Ia lost in the edges – i.e., those with peak brightness
near the beginning and end of the 2-year HLTDS-CC. Simulations
show that ≈ 250 high-z SN Ia can actually be recovered by slightly
reducing the cadence (30-60 days) just before and after the HLTDSCC.
K-Band Imaging: Several White Papers advocated for the addition

of K-band (F213) to the HLTDS (e.g., Moriya, Fox). By extending the
wavelength coverage to the reddest band possible with Roman, several exciting scientific opportunities are opened. Fox describes how
by increasing the redshift horizon accessible for Roman SNe, it may
be possible to test Initial Mass Function (IMF) evolution scenarios,
as well as constrain the contribution of massive stars to the reionization of the Universe. Moriya demonstrates that while the HLTDS will
detect PISNe out to z > 6, distinguishing them from the much larger
population of more mundane SNe will be greatly facilitated by the

Full-Filter Imaging of the Deep Tier: After stacking of the full

data set, the Roman HLTDS Deep Imaging Tier will likely be the
deepest integrations of any area on the sky obtained by Roman, offering a multitude of opportunities for static galaxy science. However,
the HLTDS-CC Deep Imaging Tier only utilizes the Z, Y, J, H, and F
filters. In the Joshi White Paper, the authors argue for the multiple
benefits of obtaining observations of this tier in all broadband Roman filters43 to improve our understanding of star formation and
quiescence at cosmic noon, as well as to improve the accuracy and

43

filter.
